<a id='question-069'></a>

### Question 069: Glycogen Storage Disease Type VII (Tarui Disease)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of Glycogen Storage Disease Type VII (Tarui disease), caused by a deficiency in muscle phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK-M), its clinical presentation mimicking McArdle disease (GSD V) but with additional features like hemolytic anemia, and the underlying biochemical mechanism. (考察糖原贮积症VII型（Tarui病），由肌肉磷酸果糖激酶-1（PFK-M）缺乏引起，其临床表现类似于McArdle病（GSD V型），但具有溶血性贫血等额外特征，以及潜在的生化机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old male construction worker presents to the emergency department complaining of severe muscle cramps, pain, and dark urine (cola-colored) after strenuous physical activity. He reports similar episodes occurring since adolescence, often triggered by intense exercise. His past medical history is unremarkable, and he denies any known allergies or medications. Family history reveals that his maternal grandfather also experienced similar symptoms. Physical examination shows normal vital signs at rest but reveals muscle tenderness upon palpation. Laboratory investigations show elevated serum creatine kinase (CK) levels (15,000 U/L, normal <200 U/L) during an episode, elevated lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) (800 U/L, normal <250 U/L), and mild anemia (hemoglobin 12.5 g/dL, normal 13.5-17.5 g/dL) with evidence of hemolysis on peripheral blood smear (schistocytes). Muscle biopsy reveals normal glycogen stores but markedly reduced activity of phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK-1) in muscle tissue. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *PFKM* gene, which encodes the muscle-specific isoform of PFK-1.

Which of the following biochemical mechanisms best explains the patient's symptoms and laboratory findings?

(A) Deficiency of muscle glycogen phosphorylase leading to impaired glycogen breakdown and accumulation of glucose-6-phosphate, causing hemolytic anemia due to oxidative stress.
(B) Deficiency of muscle phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK-M) leading to impaired glycolysis in muscle, resulting in ATP depletion, muscle cramps, rhabdomyolysis, and secondary hemolysis due to ATP deficiency in erythrocytes.
(C) Deficiency of muscle pyruvate kinase leading to impaired glycolysis, accumulation of pyruvate, and increased lactate production, causing muscle fatigue and lactic acidosis, but without significant hemolysis.
(D) Deficiency of muscle glycogen synthase leading to impaired glycogen synthesis, resulting in reduced glycogen stores and exercise intolerance, but without muscle cramps or hemolysis.
(E) Deficiency of muscle hexokinase leading to impaired glucose phosphorylation, reduced ATP production, and muscle weakness, but typically presenting in infancy with hepatomegaly and hypoglycemia.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 28-year-old male construction worker (strenuous activity).
- **Chief Complaint:** Muscle cramps, pain, dark urine after exercise (cola-colored).
- **History:** Episodes since adolescence, triggered by intense exercise, maternal grandfather with similar symptoms (suggests possible autosomal recessive inheritance).
- **Physical Exam:** Muscle tenderness.
- **Lab Findings:**
    - Elevated CK (15,000 U/L): Indicates muscle damage (rhabdomyolysis).
    - Elevated LDH (800 U/L): Indicates tissue damage, including muscle and potentially red blood cells.
    - Mild anemia (Hb 12.5 g/dL) with schistocytes: Suggests hemolytic anemia.
    - Muscle biopsy: Normal glycogen stores, markedly reduced PFK-1 activity in muscle.
    - Genetic testing: Homozygous mutation in *PFKM* gene (muscle PFK-1).
- **Deduction:** The constellation of exercise-induced muscle cramps, rhabdomyolysis (high CK), and hemolytic anemia, coupled with reduced muscle PFK-1 activity and a *PFKM* gene mutation, points strongly towards Glycogen Storage Disease Type VII (Tarui disease). The key is understanding how PFK-M deficiency causes both muscle and red blood cell problems.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Glycogen Storage Disease Type VII (Tarui Disease):** Caused by deficiency of the muscle-specific isoform of phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK-M), encoded by the *PFKM* gene. PFK-1 is a key regulatory enzyme in glycolysis, catalyzing the phosphorylation of fructose-6-phosphate to fructose-1,6-bisphosphate, using ATP.
- **Mechanism in Muscle:** During intense exercise, muscle relies heavily on anaerobic glycolysis for rapid ATP production. PFK-M deficiency impairs this pathway, leading to a bottleneck at the PFK-1 step. This results in insufficient ATP generation to meet the high energy demands of muscle contraction, causing muscle cramps, pain, fatigue, and rhabdomyolysis (muscle cell breakdown, hence high CK).
- **Mechanism in Red Blood Cells (Hemolysis):** Erythrocytes lack mitochondria and rely entirely on glycolysis for ATP production to maintain cell shape, membrane integrity, and ion gradients. ATP is crucial for the function of ion pumps (e.g., Na+/K+-ATPase, Ca2+-ATPase) and maintaining the flexibility of the red blood cell membrane. PFK-M deficiency reduces ATP production in erythrocytes, impairing these functions. This leads to decreased membrane flexibility, increased fragility, and eventual hemolysis, especially under conditions of increased ATP demand or oxidative stress (like during exercise). The presence of schistocytes on the peripheral smear is characteristic of microangiopathic hemolytic anemia or mechanical damage to red blood cells, consistent with the fragile RBCs in PFK-M deficiency.
- **Inheritance:** Typically autosomal recessive, consistent with the family history (maternal grandfather affected).
- **Distinction from McArdle Disease (GSD V):** Both cause exercise intolerance and muscle cramps. McArdle is due to muscle glycogen phosphorylase deficiency, impairing glycogen breakdown. Tarui disease impairs glycolysis *after* glycogen breakdown (or glucose uptake). Both can cause high CK and myoglobinuria. However, the hemolytic anemia is a characteristic feature distinguishing Tarui disease.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Deficiency of muscle glycogen phosphorylase (GSD V, McArdle disease). This impairs glycogen breakdown, leading to reduced glucose-1-phosphate and subsequently glucose-6-phosphate availability for glycolysis. While it causes exercise intolerance, muscle cramps, and rhabdomyolysis (high CK), it does *not* typically cause hemolytic anemia. The vignette specifically mentions reduced PFK-1 activity, not phosphorylase.
- (C): Deficiency of muscle pyruvate kinase (GSD X). This enzyme catalyzes the final step of glycolysis, converting phosphoenolpyruvate to pyruvate. Deficiency leads to impaired ATP production, muscle fatigue, and hemolytic anemia (due to reduced ATP in RBCs). However, it typically presents with *more* severe hemolytic anemia than Tarui disease and often involves neurological symptoms. The vignette points specifically to PFK-1 deficiency, not pyruvate kinase.
- (D): Deficiency of muscle glycogen synthase (GSD XIII). This impairs glycogen synthesis, leading to reduced glycogen stores. Patients experience exercise intolerance and fasting hypoglycemia, but typically *do not* have muscle cramps or hemolysis. The vignette shows normal glycogen stores and reduced PFK-1 activity.
- (E): Deficiency of muscle hexokinase (GSD XI). This enzyme phosphorylates glucose to glucose-6-phosphate, the first step of glycolysis. Deficiency impairs glucose utilization, leading to exercise intolerance. However, it usually presents in infancy with hepatomegaly, hypoglycemia, and cataracts, not typically with exercise-induced cramps and hemolysis in adulthood. The vignette points to PFK-1 deficiency.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Glycogen Storage Disease Type VII (Tarui disease) is caused by muscle PFK-1 deficiency, leading to impaired glycolysis in muscle during exercise, resulting in muscle cramps, rhabdomyolysis, and characteristic hemolytic anemia due to reduced ATP in erythrocytes. Differentiate it from McArdle disease (GSD V, phosphorylase deficiency) which lacks hemolysis, and GSD X (pyruvate kinase deficiency) which typically causes more severe hemolysis. Remember the key enzyme affected (PFK-M) and the dual impact on muscle and red blood cells.

<a id='question-070'></a>

### Question 070: Glycogen Storage Disease Type 0
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Glycogen Storage Disease Type 0 (GSD0) is caused by a deficiency in glycogen synthase, leading to impaired glycogen synthesis. This results in fasting hypoglycemia (due to inability to store glucose as glycogen) and postprandial hyperglycemia (due to inability to store ingested glucose). (GSD0 由糖原合成酶缺乏引起，导致糖原合成受损。这导致空腹低血糖（无法将葡萄糖储存为糖原）和餐后高血糖（无法储存摄入的葡萄糖）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with lethargy, sweating, and irritability after a 6-hour fast. His parents report that he typically eats frequently throughout the day and becomes irritable if he goes too long between meals. They also note that after eating large meals, especially those high in carbohydrates, he sometimes experiences transient episodes of vomiting and appears unusually hyperactive. His past medical history is unremarkable, and there is no known family history of metabolic disorders. Physical examination reveals a thin child with normal vital signs except for a slightly elevated heart rate (110 bpm). Laboratory tests show a blood glucose level of 45 mg/dL (normal fasting range: 70-100 mg/dL) after the 6-hour fast. Two hours after a standardized high-carbohydrate meal (1.5 g/kg body weight), his blood glucose level spikes to 220 mg/dL (normal postprandial range: <140 mg/dL). A liver biopsy is performed, revealing normal glycogen stores but significantly reduced glycogen synthase activity (0.5% of normal). Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *GSS1* gene.

Which of the following biochemical mechanisms best explains the patient's postprandial hyperglycemia?

(A) Impaired gluconeogenesis leading to reduced glucose production during fasting.
(B) Defective glycogen phosphorylase activity preventing glycogen breakdown.
(C) Decreased insulin secretion in response to elevated blood glucose levels.
(D) Inability to store ingested glucose as glycogen in the liver and muscle.
(E) Increased hepatic glucose uptake due to GLUT2 transporter overexpression.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with lethargy, sweating, irritability after fasting (suggests hypoglycemia). Irritability between meals, vomiting/hyperactivity after large carbohydrate meals (suggests postprandial hyperglycemia).
- **Lab Findings:** Fasting hypoglycemia (45 mg/dL) and postprandial hyperglycemia (220 mg/dL) after a glucose load. This pattern is characteristic of impaired glucose storage.
- **Liver Biopsy:** Normal glycogen stores (rules out GSDs involving breakdown defects like Von Gierke's or Cori's disease, or defects in glycogen structure like Andersen's disease). Significantly reduced glycogen synthase activity confirms a defect in glycogen synthesis.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in *GSS1* gene confirms Glycogen Storage Disease Type 0 (GSD0), which encodes liver glycogen synthase.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism explaining *postprandial hyperglycemia*.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **GSD0 Pathophysiology:** GSD0 is caused by deficiency of glycogen synthase, the enzyme responsible for the rate-limiting step in glycogen synthesis (UDP-glucose:glycogen glucosyltransferase). Glycogen synthase requires UDP-glucose as a substrate and is activated by glucose-6-phosphate (G6P) and inhibited by hormones like glucagon and epinephrine (via phosphorylation).
- **Fasting Hypoglycemia:** In the fasting state, the liver normally breaks down glycogen (glycogenolysis) to maintain blood glucose levels. In GSD0, glycogen stores are minimal or absent because synthesis is impaired. Therefore, the liver cannot effectively release glucose from glycogen stores during fasting, leading to hypoglycemia.
- **Postprandial Hyperglycemia:** After a meal, especially one rich in carbohydrates, glucose is absorbed into the bloodstream. The liver normally takes up this excess glucose (via GLUT2 transporters) and stores it as glycogen (glycogenesis). In GSD0, the defective glycogen synthase cannot efficiently convert glucose into glycogen. Consequently, ingested glucose cannot be stored, leading to a buildup of glucose in the bloodstream and causing postprandial hyperglycemia. The liver continues to take up glucose, but it cannot be effectively stored, leading to hyperglycemia.
- **Molecular Mechanism:** The *GSS1* gene encodes the catalytic subunit of liver glycogen synthase. Mutations in this gene lead to reduced enzyme activity, impairing the conversion of glucose units into glycogen chains. The inability to store glucose as glycogen after a meal directly causes the observed postprandial hyperglycemia.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Impaired gluconeogenesis leading to reduced glucose production during fasting. Gluconeogenesis is the *synthesis* of glucose from non-carbohydrate precursors (like lactate, alanine, glycerol). While impaired gluconeogenesis can cause fasting hypoglycemia (e.g., in GSD Type I, where G6Pase deficiency impairs both glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis), it does not directly explain *postprandial* hyperglycemia. In GSD0, gluconeogenesis is typically intact. The primary defect is in glycogen synthesis, not gluconeogenesis.
- (B) Defective glycogen phosphorylase activity preventing glycogen breakdown. Defective glycogen phosphorylase causes GSD Type V (McArdle disease) or GSD Type VI (Hers disease), which primarily affect muscle (Type V) or liver (Type VI). These diseases impair glycogen *breakdown* (glycogenolysis), leading to fasting hypoglycemia (due to inability to release glucose from glycogen) and exercise intolerance (Type V). They do not cause postprandial hyperglycemia; rather, they might cause hypoglycemia after exercise or fasting. The vignette describes normal glycogen stores, ruling out defects in glycogen breakdown.
- (C) Decreased insulin secretion in response to elevated blood glucose levels. Decreased insulin secretion would worsen hyperglycemia, but it is not the primary mechanism in GSD0. In GSD0, the hyperglycemia occurs because glucose cannot be stored as glycogen. Insulin secretion is typically normal or even elevated in response to the high glucose levels, attempting to compensate by promoting glucose uptake into peripheral tissues. The underlying problem is the inability of the liver to store the glucose.
- (E) Increased hepatic glucose uptake due to GLUT2 transporter overexpression. GLUT2 is the primary glucose transporter in the liver, responsible for bidirectional glucose transport (uptake after a meal, release during fasting). Its activity is not typically increased in GSD0. While the liver does take up glucose after a meal, the problem is not increased uptake but rather the inability to store the taken-up glucose as glycogen. Overexpression of GLUT2 is not a feature of GSD0.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Glycogen Storage Disease Type 0 (GSD0) is characterized by defective glycogen synthase, leading to impaired glycogen synthesis. This results in fasting hypoglycemia (due to inability to store glucose) and postprandial hyperglycemia (due to inability to store ingested glucose). Key differentials include GSD Type I (Von Gierke, G6Pase deficiency - fasting hypoglycemia, lactic acidosis, hyperlipidemia, hepatomegaly), GSD Type III (Cori, debranching enzyme deficiency - fasting hypoglycemia, hepatomegaly, milder than Type I), GSD Type IV (Andersen, branching enzyme deficiency - hepatosplenomegaly, cirrhosis, normal fasting glucose), GSD Type V (McArdle, muscle phosphorylase deficiency - exercise intolerance, muscle cramps), and GSD Type VI (Hers, liver phosphorylase deficiency - hepatomegaly, mild fasting hypoglycemia). The unique pattern of fasting hypoglycemia and postprandial hyperglycemia points specifically to GSD0.

<a id='question-071'></a>

### Question 071: Glycogen Storage Disease Type I (von Gierke Disease)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the biochemical defect in Glycogen Storage Disease Type Ia (von Gierke disease), specifically the deficiency of glucose-6-phosphatase (G6Pase), its impact on glucose homeostasis, and the resulting clinical manifestations, including the effect of fructose administration. (考察糖原累积症I型(von Gierke病)的生化缺陷，特别是葡萄糖-6-磷酸酶(G6Pase)的缺乏，其对葡萄糖稳态的影响，以及由此产生的临床表现，包括果糖给药的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with severe hypoglycemia (blood glucose 35 mg/dL), lactic acidosis (lactate 8 mmol/L), and hyperlipidemia (triglycerides 800 mg/dL). He has a history of recurrent hypoglycemic episodes, especially during fasting, and hepatomegaly noted on physical examination. His parents report that he has poor growth and exercise intolerance. Laboratory testing reveals markedly elevated levels of glycogen in the liver and kidneys. A genetic test confirms a homozygous mutation in the *G6PC* gene. The physician decides to administer fructose intravenously to manage the hypoglycemia. Which of the following biochemical mechanisms explains why fructose administration can transiently improve blood glucose levels in this patient?

(A) Fructose is directly phosphorylated by glucokinase in the liver, bypassing the rate-limiting step of glycolysis and increasing ATP production, which stimulates gluconeogenesis.
(B) Fructose is metabolized via fructokinase to fructose-1-phosphate, which allosterically activates phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK-1) in glycolysis, leading to increased pyruvate production and subsequent gluconeogenesis.
(C) Fructose is metabolized via fructokinase to fructose-1-phosphate, which is then cleaved by aldolase B into glyceraldehyde and dihydroxyacetone phosphate (DHAP). Glyceraldehyde is phosphorylated to glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate, and DHAP enters gluconeogenesis, providing substrates for glucose synthesis.
(D) Fructose is metabolized via fructokinase to fructose-1-phosphate, which inhibits glycogen synthase, preventing further glycogen breakdown and conserving glucose precursors.
(E) Fructose is metabolized via fructokinase to fructose-1-phosphate, which activates glycogen phosphorylase, leading to increased glycogenolysis and release of glucose into the bloodstream.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with recurrent hypoglycemia, lactic acidosis, hyperlipidemia, hepatomegaly, poor growth, exercise intolerance. These are classic signs of Glycogen Storage Disease Type I (von Gierke disease).
- **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated liver and kidney glycogen, severe hypoglycemia, lactic acidosis, hyperlipidemia. These confirm a defect in glycogenolysis or gluconeogenesis.
- **Genetic Test:** Homozygous mutation in the *G6PC* gene confirms Glycogen Storage Disease Type Ia, caused by deficiency of the catalytic subunit of glucose-6-phosphatase (G6Pase).
- **Pathophysiology of G6Pase Deficiency:** G6Pase is located in the endoplasmic reticulum and is essential for the final step of both glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis, converting glucose-6-phosphate (G6P) to free glucose. Deficiency prevents the release of free glucose into the bloodstream from the liver and kidneys, leading to hypoglycemia. The accumulation of G6P inhibits glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis, leading to glycogen accumulation and increased flux through glycolysis and the pentose phosphate pathway, causing lactic acidosis and hyperlipidemia.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism by which fructose administration can *transiently* improve blood glucose in this patient. This implies fructose metabolism bypasses the defective G6Pase step.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **G6Pase Deficiency (GSD Type Ia):** The enzyme glucose-6-phosphatase (G6Pase) is a multi-subunit enzyme complex (G6PCα catalytic subunit, G6PCβ regulatory subunit, and a third subunit UQCRC2) located in the endoplasmic reticulum of hepatocytes and renal tubular cells. It hydrolyzes glucose-6-phosphate (G6P) to glucose and inorganic phosphate (Pi). This is the final step in both glycogenolysis (breakdown of glycogen to glucose) and gluconeogenesis (synthesis of glucose from non-carbohydrate precursors). Deficiency prevents the release of free glucose into the circulation, causing severe fasting hypoglycemia.
- **Fructose Metabolism:** Fructose is primarily metabolized in the liver.
    1.  **Phosphorylation:** Fructose is phosphorylated by fructokinase (ketohexokinase, KHK) to fructose-1-phosphate (F-1-P). This step uses ATP.
    2.  **Cleavage:** F-1-P is cleaved by aldolase B (ALDOB) into glyceraldehyde and dihydroxyacetone phosphate (DHAP).
    3.  **Further Metabolism:**
        *   DHAP is an intermediate in glycolysis and gluconeogenesis. It can be isomerized to glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate (G3P) by triose phosphate isomerase.
        *   Glyceraldehyde is phosphorylated by triokinase (glyceraldehyde kinase) to glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate (G3P).
        *   Both G3P and DHAP can then enter the gluconeogenic pathway, ultimately leading to the synthesis of glucose-6-phosphate (G6P).
- **Bypassing G6Pase:** Since the defect is in G6Pase, the pathway must bypass the conversion of G6P to glucose. Fructose metabolism provides intermediates (DHAP and G3P) that can enter the gluconeogenic pathway *before* the G6Pase step. These intermediates are converted to G6P, which accumulates due to the enzyme deficiency. However, the *net* effect of fructose metabolism is the production of substrates that can be used for gluconeogenesis, leading to the synthesis of more G6P, which, although it cannot be converted to free glucose, can still contribute to the overall metabolic flux and potentially transiently improve glucose homeostasis by providing substrates for other pathways or reducing the reliance on glycogen stores. The key is that fructose metabolism generates intermediates *upstream* of the G6Pase block.
- **Why Fructose Works (Transiently):** Fructose metabolism generates DHAP and glyceraldehyde, which are precursors for gluconeogenesis. These precursors enter the gluconeogenic pathway, leading to the formation of G6P. Although G6P cannot be converted to free glucose due to the G6Pase deficiency, the influx of carbon from fructose metabolism can temporarily increase the pool of gluconeogenic precursors and potentially alleviate the severity of hypoglycemia, even if only transiently. The effect is transient because the accumulated G6P eventually inhibits upstream enzymes in gluconeogenesis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** Glucokinase phosphorylates glucose, not fructose. Fructose is phosphorylated by fructokinase. Also, glucokinase is not the rate-limiting step of glycolysis (PFK-1 is); it's important for glucose sensing in the liver and pancreas. Furthermore, increased ATP production would inhibit, not stimulate, gluconeogenesis.
- (B): **Incorrect.** Fructose is metabolized by fructokinase, not directly phosphorylated by glucokinase. Fructose-1-phosphate does not allosterically activate PFK-1. In fact, high levels of F-1-P can inhibit glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis in GSD Type I.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Fructose metabolism does not inhibit glycogen synthase. Glycogen synthase is inhibited by phosphorylation (activated by dephosphorylation). Fructose metabolism generates precursors for gluconeogenesis, not inhibitors of glycogen synthesis.
- (E): **Incorrect.** Fructose metabolism does not activate glycogen phosphorylase. Glycogen phosphorylase is activated by phosphorylation (stimulated by glucagon/epinephrine) and inhibited by G6P and ATP. Fructose metabolism bypasses the need for glycogenolysis by providing gluconeogenic precursors.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Understand the biochemical defect in GSD Type Ia (G6Pase deficiency) and how it affects glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis, leading to hypoglycemia. Recognize that fructose metabolism bypasses the G6Pase step by providing gluconeogenic precursors (DHAP, G3P).
- **Differentials:** GSD Type Ib (deficiency of G6PCβ subunit, also causes neutropenia), GSD Type III (debranching enzyme deficiency), GSD Type VI (liver phosphorylase deficiency). Understand the specific enzyme defects and metabolic consequences of each type. Also, differentiate from other causes of hypoglycemia (insulinoma, adrenal insufficiency, etc.).

---

<a id='question-072'></a>

### Question 072: von Gierke Disease (Glycogen Storage Disease Type Ia)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Deficient glucose-6-phosphatase activity in von Gierke disease (GSD Ia) impairs both glycogenolysis (releasing glucose from glycogen stores) and gluconeogenesis (synthesizing glucose from non-carbohydrate precursors), leading to severe fasting hypoglycemia, hepatomegaly, lactic acidosis, and hyperlipidemia. (von Gierke 病/糖原累积症 Ia 型中葡萄糖-6-磷酸酶活性不足，损害了糖原异化作用（从糖原储存中释放葡萄糖）和葡萄糖新合成作用（从非碳水化合物前体合成葡萄糖），导致严重的空腹低血糖、肝脏肿大、乳酸酸中毒和高脂血症。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with lethargy, seizures, and diaphoresis after fasting overnight. His mother reports that he typically eats frequently throughout the day and becomes irritable if he goes too long between meals. Physical examination reveals a distended abdomen with hepatomegaly, and his face appears cherubic. Laboratory investigations show severe hypoglycemia (glucose 25 mg/dL), elevated lactate (7 mmol/L), hypertriglyceridemia (triglycerides 800 mg/dL), and hyperuricemia (uric acid 10 mg/dL). A liver biopsy demonstrates excessive glycogen accumulation. Enzyme activity assays in cultured skin fibroblasts show markedly reduced glucose-6-phosphatase activity. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *G6PC* gene.

Which of the following biochemical pathways is MOST directly impaired in this patient, explaining the severe fasting hypoglycemia?
(A) Glycolysis
(B) Beta-oxidation of fatty acids
(C) Gluconeogenesis
(D) Glycogen synthesis
(E) Pentose phosphate pathway

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Age and Presentation:** A 3-year-old boy with lethargy, seizures, and diaphoresis after fasting suggests severe hypoglycemia.
- **Eating Pattern:** Frequent eating and irritability between meals point towards an inability to maintain blood glucose levels during fasting.
- **Physical Exam:** Hepatomegaly (distended abdomen) and cherubic facies are characteristic features of glycogen storage diseases, particularly von Gierke disease.
- **Lab Findings:**
    - Severe hypoglycemia (glucose 25 mg/dL): Confirms the primary metabolic disturbance.
    - Elevated lactate (7 mmol/L): Indicates impaired gluconeogenesis, as lactate is a major gluconeogenic substrate. Also suggests increased anaerobic glycolysis due to lack of glucose utilization.
    - Hypertriglyceridemia (triglycerides 800 mg/dL): Results from increased fatty acid synthesis and decreased fatty acid oxidation due to high insulin levels (stimulated by hypoglycemia) and substrate availability.
    - Hyperuricemia (uric acid 10 mg/dL): Caused by increased purine nucleotide degradation and decreased renal excretion, both related to increased substrate availability and metabolic flux.
- **Liver Biopsy:** Excessive glycogen accumulation confirms a glycogen storage disease.
- **Enzyme Assay:** Markedly reduced glucose-6-phosphatase activity specifically identifies the enzyme defect.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in the *G6PC* gene confirms the diagnosis of von Gierke disease (GSD Ia), which encodes glucose-6-phosphatase.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST directly impaired biochemical pathway explaining the severe fasting hypoglycemia.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Glucose-6-phosphatase (G6Pase):** This enzyme is located primarily in the endoplasmic reticulum of hepatocytes and kidney proximal tubule cells. Its function is to hydrolyze glucose-6-phosphate (G6P) to free glucose and inorganic phosphate (Pi).
- **Role in Glycogenolysis:** During glycogenolysis, glycogen phosphorylase breaks down glycogen into glucose-1-phosphate. Phosphoglucomutase converts glucose-1-phosphate to glucose-6-phosphate. G6Pase then dephosphorylates G6P to release free glucose into the bloodstream, maintaining blood glucose levels, especially during fasting. Deficiency prevents this final step.
- **Role in Gluconeogenesis:** Gluconeogenesis synthesizes glucose from precursors like lactate, pyruvate, glycerol, and certain amino acids. The final step of gluconeogenesis involves the conversion of fructose-6-phosphate to fructose-1,6-bisphosphate, followed by the formation of fructose-1,6-bisphosphate aldolase products (dihydroxyacetone phosphate and glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate). Dihydroxyacetone phosphate is converted to glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate, which is then phosphorylated to 1,3-bisphosphoglycerate, eventually leading to the formation of glucose-6-phosphate. G6Pase is required to dephosphorylate this G6P to release free glucose. Deficiency blocks the final step of gluconeogenesis.
- **Pathophysiology of von Gierke Disease (GSD Ia):** The deficiency of G6Pase impairs both glycogenolysis (final step) and gluconeogenesis (final step). This prevents the liver from releasing glucose into the blood during fasting, leading to severe hypoglycemia. The accumulation of G6P inhibits glycogen phosphorylase (feedback inhibition) and promotes glycogen synthesis, leading to massive glycogen accumulation in the liver and kidneys (hepatomegaly, nephromegaly). The buildup of G6P also shunts carbon into alternative pathways:
    - Increased glycolysis leads to elevated lactate (lactic acidosis).
    - Increased pentose phosphate pathway activity leads to increased NADPH and ribose-5-phosphate production.
    - Increased fatty acid synthesis leads to hypertriglyceridemia.
    - Increased purine synthesis and degradation leads to hyperuricemia.
- **Conclusion:** The deficiency of glucose-6-phosphatase directly impairs the final step of both glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis, preventing the release of free glucose into the circulation and causing severe fasting hypoglycemia. Therefore, gluconeogenesis is a pathway MOST directly impaired.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Glycolysis: Glycolysis is the breakdown of glucose to pyruvate. While increased glycolysis occurs in von Gierke disease due to G6P accumulation, it is a consequence of the G6Pase deficiency, not the primary pathway directly impaired in its final step. The *block* is in releasing glucose, not in breaking it down.
- (B) Beta-oxidation of fatty acids: Beta-oxidation is the breakdown of fatty acids. In von Gierke disease, beta-oxidation is actually *inhibited* due to high levels of malonyl-CoA (resulting from increased fatty acid synthesis driven by high insulin and substrate availability) and potentially due to the accumulation of G6P intermediates affecting mitochondrial function. It is not directly impaired by the G6Pase defect itself.
- (D) Glycogen synthesis: Glycogen synthesis (glycogenesis) is *increased* in von Gierke disease due to the accumulation of G6P, which allosterically activates glycogen synthase and inhibits glycogen phosphorylase. The enzyme defect is in glycogen breakdown (glycogenolysis) and gluconeogenesis, not in synthesis.
- (E) Pentose phosphate pathway: The pentose phosphate pathway (PPP) is *activated* in von Gierke disease due to the accumulation of G6P, which is a substrate for the first enzyme, glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase. This increased flux through the PPP contributes to hyperlipidemia (via NADPH production) and hyperuricemia (via increased purine synthesis). It is not the pathway directly impaired by the G6Pase deficiency.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Von Gierke disease (GSD Ia) is caused by deficient glucose-6-phosphatase, impairing the final steps of both glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis, leading to severe fasting hypoglycemia, hepatomegaly, lactic acidosis, hyperlipidemia, and hyperuricemia.
- **Differentials:** Other glycogen storage diseases (GSDs) affect different enzymes in glycogen metabolism. GSD III (Cori disease) involves debranching enzyme deficiency, GSD V (McArdle disease) involves muscle glycogen phosphorylase deficiency, GSD IX (liver phosphorylase deficiency) involves liver glycogen phosphorylase deficiency. These primarily affect glycogenolysis but not gluconeogenesis to the same extent as GSD Ia. GSD Ia is unique in impairing both pathways via the G6Pase defect.

---

<a id='question-073'></a>

### Question 073: Glycogen Storage Disease Type Ia (von Gierke Disease)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the biochemical defect, clinical manifestations, and underlying pathophysiology of Glycogen Storage Disease Type Ia (GSD Ia), specifically focusing on the role of glucose-6-phosphatase deficiency in causing hypoglycemia, lactic acidosis, hyperuricemia, and hyperlipidemia. (考察糖原累积症Ia型（冯·吉尔克病）的生化缺陷、临床表现和潜在病理生理机制，特别是葡萄糖-6-磷酸酶缺乏导致低血糖、乳酸增多症、高尿酸血症和高脂血症。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with recurrent episodes of hypoglycemia, particularly during fasting. His parents report that he has a characteristic "doll-like" facial appearance with frontal bossing and cherubic features. Physical examination reveals massive hepatomegaly, extending significantly below the costal margin, and renomegaly. Laboratory investigations show severe hypoglycemia (glucose 35 mg/dL), markedly elevated lactate levels (12 mmol/L), hyperuricemia (uric acid 10 mg/dL), and hypertriglyceridemia (triglycerides 800 mg/dL). A liver biopsy shows excessive glycogen deposition. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *G6PC* gene. Which of the following biochemical mechanisms best explains the hyperuricemia observed in this patient?

(A) Increased activity of hepatic fructokinase leading to increased fructose-1-phosphate levels, which inhibits glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis.
(B) Decreased activity of hepatic glucose-6-phosphatase, leading to accumulation of glucose-6-phosphate, which inhibits glycogenolysis and increases the flux of ribose-5-phosphate into the pentose phosphate pathway, increasing PRPP synthesis.
(C) Increased activity of hepatic adenylate cyclase, leading to increased cAMP levels, which stimulates glycogen phosphorylase and inhibits glycogen synthase.
(D) Decreased activity of hepatic phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK-1), leading to accumulation of fructose-6-phosphate, which inhibits glycolysis and increases the flux of glucose-6-phosphate into the pentose phosphate pathway.
(E) Increased activity of hepatic glucokinase, leading to increased glucose phosphorylation and depletion of intracellular glucose, further exacerbating hypoglycemia.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with recurrent hypoglycemia, doll-like facies, massive hepatomegaly, renomegaly. These are classic signs of GSD Ia (von Gierke disease).
- **Laboratory Findings:** Severe hypoglycemia (35 mg/dL), elevated lactate (12 mmol/L), hyperuricemia (10 mg/dL), hypertriglyceridemia (800 mg/dL). These metabolic derangements are characteristic of GSD Ia. Hypoglycemia results from impaired glucose release from the liver. Lactic acidosis results from the inability to convert lactate back to glucose via gluconeogenesis and the shunting of pyruvate to lactate. Hyperlipidemia results from increased fatty acid synthesis and decreased fatty acid oxidation due to high malonyl-CoA levels (secondary to impaired gluconeogenesis).
- **Liver Biopsy:** Excessive glycogen deposition confirms a glycogen storage disease.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in the *G6PC* gene confirms GSD Ia, caused by deficiency of glucose-6-phosphatase.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the biochemical mechanism explaining the *hyperuricemia*.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **GSD Ia Pathophysiology:** GSD Ia is caused by a deficiency in glucose-6-phosphatase (G6Pase), an enzyme primarily located in the endoplasmic reticulum of hepatocytes and kidney proximal tubules. This enzyme is crucial for the final step of both gluconeogenesis and glycogenolysis, which is the hydrolysis of glucose-6-phosphate (G6P) to free glucose and inorganic phosphate (Pi).
- **Consequences of G6Pase Deficiency:**
    - **Hypoglycemia:** The inability to release free glucose from the liver leads to severe fasting hypoglycemia.
    - **Lactic Acidosis:** Pyruvate cannot be converted to glucose via gluconeogenesis. Instead, it is converted to lactate by lactate dehydrogenase (LDH), leading to lactic acidosis.
    - **Hyperlipidemia:** Impaired gluconeogenesis leads to increased levels of malonyl-CoA, which inhibits carnitine palmitoyltransferase I (CPT-I), blocking fatty acid oxidation. This, combined with increased fatty acid synthesis, results in hypertriglyceridemia.
    - **Hyperuricemia:** This is the key point for the question. The deficiency of G6Pase leads to the accumulation of G6P within the liver cells. This accumulation has several metabolic consequences:
        1.  **Inhibition of Glycogenolysis/Gluconeogenesis:** High levels of G6P allosterically inhibit key enzymes in both pathways, including glycogen phosphorylase and fructose-1,6-bisphosphatase, further impairing glucose production.
        2.  **Increased Pentose Phosphate Pathway (PPP) Flux:** The excess G6P is shunted into the PPP. The first step of the PPP is the conversion of G6P to 6-phosphogluconolactone by G6P dehydrogenase. This reaction produces NADPH. The subsequent step, catalyzed by 6-phosphogluconolactonase, produces 6-phosphogluconate. The third step, catalyzed by 6-phosphogluconate dehydrogenase, produces ribulose-5-phosphate and NADPH, and importantly, releases CO2. This step is irreversible and committed. The ribulose-5-phosphate is then epimerized to ribose-5-phosphate and subsequently converted to ribose-1-phosphate by phosphoribosyl pyrophosphate (PRPP) synthetase.
        3.  **Increased PRPP Synthesis:** The increased flux through the PPP leads to an overproduction of ribose-5-phosphate. Ribose-5-phosphate is a substrate for PRPP synthetase, which catalyzes the formation of PRPP from ribose-5-phosphate and ATP.
        4.  **Increased Purine Synthesis:** PRPP is a key activator of the enzyme amidophosphoribosyltransferase (ATCase), the first committed step in *de novo* purine biosynthesis. Increased PRPP levels lead to increased purine synthesis.
        5.  **Increased Purine Degradation:** The increased rate of purine synthesis leads to increased purine turnover and degradation. The end product of purine degradation in humans is uric acid. Therefore, the accumulation of G6P due to G6Pase deficiency leads to increased uric acid production and subsequent hyperuricemia.

- **Mechanism Linking G6Pase Deficiency to Hyperuricemia:** G6Pase deficiency → Accumulation of G6P → Increased flux through PPP → Increased Ribose-5-P → Increased PRPP → Increased Purine Synthesis → Increased Purine Degradation → Increased Uric Acid Production → Hyperuricemia.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Increased fructokinase activity is associated with essential fructosuria, not GSD Ia. Fructokinase phosphorylates fructose to fructose-1-phosphate. While fructose metabolism can influence glucose homeostasis, this is not the primary mechanism for hyperuricemia in GSD Ia. Fructose-1-phosphate accumulation can inhibit glycogenolysis, but this is not the main cause of hyperuricemia.
- (C): Increased adenylate cyclase activity leads to increased cAMP, which activates protein kinase A (PKA). PKA phosphorylates and activates glycogen phosphorylase and inhibits glycogen synthase. This describes the hormonal regulation of glycogen metabolism (e.g., by glucagon or epinephrine), not the underlying cause of hyperuricemia in GSD Ia.
- (D): Decreased PFK-1 activity would impair glycolysis, leading to accumulation of fructose-6-phosphate and potentially G6P. However, PFK-1 deficiency is not characteristic of GSD Ia. Furthermore, while increased PPP flux occurs in GSD Ia, the primary defect is G6Pase deficiency, not PFK-1 deficiency. PFK-1 deficiency would likely cause hemolytic anemia.
- (E): Increased glucokinase activity would increase glucose phosphorylation, potentially worsening hypoglycemia by trapping glucose in cells. Glucokinase is primarily found in the liver and pancreas. While its activity is relevant to glucose metabolism, increased activity is not the cause of GSD Ia, nor does it directly explain the hyperuricemia. In fact, GSD Ia is characterized by *decreased* glucose release, not increased glucose trapping by glucokinase.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
GSD Ia (von Gierke disease) is caused by glucose-6-phosphatase deficiency, leading to impaired glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis. This results in hypoglycemia, lactic acidosis, hyperuricemia (due to increased PRPP synthesis via the pentose phosphate pathway), and hyperlipidemia. Remember the key enzyme and its role in the final common step of glucose release. Differential diagnoses for hypoglycemia include other GSDs, fatty acid oxidation defects, and hormonal deficiencies. Differential diagnoses for hyperuricemia include Lesch-Nyhan syndrome (HGPRT deficiency) and PRPP synthetase overactivity.

---

<a id='question-074'></a>

### Question 074: Hyperuricemia in von Gierke Disease
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the biochemical basis for hyperuricemia in von Gierke disease (Glycogen Storage Disease Type Ia), specifically the dual mechanisms involving increased purine synthesis due to elevated G6P and decreased uric acid excretion due to competition with lactic acid. (考察 von Gierke 病 (I 型糖原病) 中高尿酸血症的生化基础，特别是涉及 G6P 升高导致嘌呤合成增加和乳酸与尿酸竞争肾小管分泌减少的双重机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with recurrent episodes of severe abdominal pain, vomiting, and lethargy, often triggered by fasting or illness. His physical examination reveals hepatomegaly, short stature, and a doll-like facies. Laboratory investigations show hypoglycemia (blood glucose 35 mg/dL), lactic acidosis (serum lactate 6 mmol/L), hyperlipidemia (triglycerides 800 mg/dL), and marked hyperuricemia (serum uric acid 12 mg/dL). A liver biopsy reveals excessive glycogen deposition. Genetic testing confirms a deficiency in glucose-6-phosphatase (G6Pase). His parents are asymptomatic carriers. The physician suspects the hyperuricemia is a direct consequence of the underlying metabolic defect. Which of the following mechanisms BEST explains the hyperuricemia in this patient?

(A) Decreased hepatic uptake of uric acid due to impaired glycogenolysis.
(B) Increased renal tubular reabsorption of uric acid secondary to lactic acidosis.
(C) Increased de novo purine synthesis driven by elevated intracellular glucose-6-phosphate (G6P) levels.
(D) Decreased activity of xanthine oxidase due to inhibition by accumulated lactate.
(E) Increased degradation of hepatic glycogen leading to increased ATP turnover and purine breakdown.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 6-year-old boy with recurrent abdominal pain, vomiting, lethargy, hepatomegaly, short stature, doll-like facies. These are classic signs of von Gierke disease (GSD Type Ia).
- **Laboratory Findings:** Hypoglycemia, lactic acidosis, hyperlipidemia, and hyperuricemia. These metabolic abnormalities are characteristic of GSD Type Ia.
- **Liver Biopsy:** Excessive glycogen deposition confirms a glycogen storage disease.
- **Genetic Testing:** Deficiency in glucose-6-phosphatase (G6Pase) confirms GSD Type Ia. G6Pase is crucial for the final step of both gluconeogenesis and glycogenolysis, releasing free glucose into the bloodstream.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism explaining hyperuricemia in this context.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **G6Pase Deficiency:** In von Gierke disease (GSD Type Ia), the deficiency of G6Pase leads to the accumulation of glucose-6-phosphate (G6P) in the liver, kidney, and intestinal mucosa.
- **Mechanism 1: Increased Purine Synthesis:** High intracellular G6P levels allosterically activate the enzyme phosphoribosyl pyrophosphate (PRPP) synthetase. PRPP is a key precursor in the de novo synthesis of purines (adenine and guanine). Increased PRPP synthesis leads to increased purine nucleotide synthesis. The breakdown of purines ultimately produces uric acid. Therefore, elevated G6P drives increased uric acid production. (G6Pase 缺乏导致 G6P 积累，G6P 全酶促动 PRPP 合成酶，增加 PRPP 水平，促进从头嘌呤合成，最终导致尿酸生成增加。)
- **Mechanism 2: Decreased Uric Acid Excretion:** The liver's inability to release free glucose leads to hypoglycemia. This triggers compensatory mechanisms, including increased glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis, which ultimately result in increased lactate production (lactic acidosis). High levels of lactate in the renal tubules compete with uric acid for secretion via organic anion transporters (OATs), primarily OAT1 and OAT3. This competition reduces the renal excretion of uric acid, contributing to hyperuricemia. (G6Pase 缺乏导致低血糖，激活糖异生和糖酵解，增加乳酸生成 (乳酸酸中毒)。高乳酸水平与尿酸竞争肾小管有机阴离子转运体 (OAT) 的分泌位点，减少尿酸排泄，加剧高尿酸血症。)
- **Combined Effect:** Both increased production (via G6P-driven purine synthesis) and decreased excretion (via lactate competition) contribute significantly to the severe hyperuricemia observed in von Gierke disease. Option (C) directly addresses the increased production mechanism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Decreased hepatic uptake of uric acid due to impaired glycogenolysis. This is incorrect. Glycogenolysis is impaired in GSD Type Ia due to the inability to release glucose from G6P, but this doesn't directly affect hepatic *uptake* of uric acid. Hepatic uptake is primarily related to OATPs, not glycogenolysis itself. Furthermore, the primary issue is decreased *excretion* by the kidney, not decreased uptake by the liver.
- (B) Increased renal tubular reabsorption of uric acid secondary to lactic acidosis. This is incorrect. Lactic acidosis leads to *decreased* renal tubular *secretion* of uric acid due to competition for the OATs, not increased reabsorption. Uric acid is primarily filtered and then secreted, with some reabsorption occurring, but the dominant effect of lactic acidosis is reduced secretion.
- (D) Decreased activity of xanthine oxidase due to inhibition by accumulated lactate. This is incorrect. Xanthine oxidase is the enzyme that catalyzes the final steps in purine degradation, converting hypoxanthine to xanthine and xanthine to uric acid. While high lactate levels can affect various metabolic processes, there is no known direct inhibition of xanthine oxidase by lactate. The increased uric acid is due to increased substrate (purines) for xanthine oxidase, not decreased enzyme activity.
- (E) Increased degradation of hepatic glycogen leading to increased ATP turnover and purine breakdown. This is incorrect. While glycogen degradation does occur, the primary defect is the inability to release free glucose from G6P. Increased ATP turnover is not a primary feature, and purine *breakdown* (leading to uric acid) is increased due to increased *synthesis* (driven by G6P), not increased degradation of glycogen itself. The increased purine synthesis is the key driver of increased uric acid production.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Von Gierke disease (GSD Type Ia) causes hyperuricemia through two main mechanisms: increased de novo purine synthesis (due to elevated G6P activating PRPP synthetase) and decreased renal excretion of uric acid (due to competition with lactic acid for tubular secretion). Remember the key enzyme deficiency (G6Pase) and its metabolic consequences (hypoglycemia, lactic acidosis, hyperlipidemia, hyperuricemia). Differential diagnoses for hyperuricemia include Lesch-Nyhan syndrome (increased purine synthesis due to HGPRT deficiency), tumor lysis syndrome, and certain medications. Differentiating GSD Type Ia involves recognizing the constellation of hypoglycemia, lactic acidosis, hyperlipidemia, and hepatomegaly alongside hyperuricemia.
