<a id='question-083'></a>

### Question 083: Andersen Disease Manifestations: Progressive Hepatosplenomegaly, Early Liver Cirrhosis, Hepatic Failure, and Death in Infancy - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of Andersen disease (Glycogen Storage Disease Type IV, GSD IV), specifically its pathophysiology related to the deficiency of the glycogen branching enzyme (GBE1), leading to the accumulation of abnormal glycogen (amylopectin-like) primarily in the liver, causing hepatosplenomegaly, cirrhosis, and early death. It also tests the ability to interpret biochemical findings related to glycogen metabolism. (安德森病（IV型糖原贮积症），其病理生理机制与糖原支化酶（GBE1）缺乏有关，导致异常糖原（淀粉样）主要在肝脏中积累，从而引起肝脾肿大、肝硬化和早夭。本题还考察了学生对糖原代谢相关生化结果的解读能力。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old male infant presents to the pediatric emergency department with progressive abdominal distension, failure to thrive, and jaundice. His parents report that he has been unusually lethargic for the past few weeks. Family history is significant for a similarly affected older brother who died at 1 year of age from liver failure. Physical examination reveals hepatosplenomegaly, ascites, and signs of chronic liver disease. Laboratory investigations show elevated liver enzymes (AST 450 U/L, ALT 380 U/L), hyperbilirubinemia (total bilirubin 5 mg/dL), hypoalbuminemia (albumin 2.2 g/dL), and prolonged prothrombin time (PT 25 seconds). A liver biopsy reveals diffuse glycogen accumulation within hepatocytes, characterized by large, abnormally structured glycogen rosettes on electron microscopy, resembling amylopectin. Enzyme activity assays show markedly reduced glycogen branching enzyme (GBE1) activity (<5% of normal). Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *AGBL5* gene. Given these findings, which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the pathogenesis of the liver disease in this patient?

(A) Impaired glucose-6-phosphatase activity leading to accumulation of glucose-6-phosphate and hepatomegaly.
(B) Deficiency in the glycogen debranching enzyme (amylo-α-1,6-glucosidase) resulting in the accumulation of limit dextrins (oligosaccharides with α-1,6 linkages).
(C) Defective glycogen branching enzyme (GBE1) leading to the synthesis of abnormal, poorly soluble glycogen with long outer chains (amylopectin-like), causing cellular distension and liver damage.
(D) Deficiency in the glycogen synthase enzyme resulting in decreased glycogen synthesis and hypoglycemia.
(E) Impaired activity of the glucose transporter GLUT2 in hepatocytes, leading to impaired glucose uptake and glycogen synthesis.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 6-month-old male with progressive abdominal distension, failure to thrive, lethargy, jaundice, hepatosplenomegaly, ascites, chronic liver disease signs. This points towards a severe liver disorder.
- **Family History:** Older brother died at 1 year from liver failure, suggesting a genetic, likely autosomal recessive, condition affecting the liver.
- **Lab Findings:** Elevated AST/ALT, hyperbilirubinemia, hypoalbuminemia, prolonged PT – all indicative of significant liver dysfunction and cirrhosis.
- **Liver Biopsy:** Diffuse glycogen accumulation in hepatocytes, abnormal glycogen rosettes resembling amylopectin on electron microscopy. This is the key finding pointing towards a defect in glycogen structure or synthesis.
- **Enzyme Assay:** Markedly reduced glycogen branching enzyme (GBE1) activity. This directly identifies the deficient enzyme.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in *AGBL5* gene, which encodes the glycogen branching enzyme (GBE1). This confirms the molecular basis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the liver disease pathogenesis.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Andersen disease (GSD IV) is caused by mutations in the *AGBL5* gene, encoding the glycogen branching enzyme (GBE1). This enzyme catalyzes the transfer of a block of 6-8 glucose residues from the non-reducing end of a linear α-1,4-linked chain to the C-6 hydroxyl group of a glucose residue on a pre-existing branch point, creating an α-1,6 linkage. Deficiency of GBE1 leads to the accumulation of abnormal glycogen with long, unbranched outer chains (limit dextrin-like structures) and fewer branch points. This abnormal glycogen (amylopectin-like) is poorly soluble and tends to accumulate within lysosomes, causing cellular distension, particularly in the liver and muscle. In the liver, this leads to progressive hepatomegaly, fibrosis, cirrhosis, and ultimately hepatic failure, often resulting in death in infancy or early childhood. The clinical presentation (early onset, severe liver disease, family history) and the specific biochemical findings (abnormal glycogen structure, deficient GBE1 activity, *AGBL5* mutation) are all consistent with Andersen disease. The abnormal glycogen structure is the direct cause of the cellular damage and organ dysfunction.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired glucose-6-phosphatase activity is characteristic of Von Gierke disease (GSD Ia). This leads to the accumulation of glucose-6-phosphate, causing hypoglycemia, hepatomegaly (due to glycogen and fat accumulation), lactic acidosis, and hyperuricemia. The glycogen structure is normal in GSD Ia. This mechanism does not fit the abnormal glycogen structure (amylopectin-like) or the specific enzyme deficiency (GBE1) described in the vignette.
- (B): Deficiency in the glycogen debranching enzyme (amylo-α-1,6-glucosidase) causes Cori disease (GSD III). This enzyme removes the branch points (α-1,6 linkages) during glycogenolysis. Deficiency leads to the accumulation of limit dextrins (oligosaccharides with α-1,6 linkages at the branch point) in the liver and muscle. While it causes hepatomegaly and hypoglycemia, the glycogen structure is different (limit dextrins, not long unbranched chains), and the deficient enzyme is different (debranching enzyme, not branching enzyme).
- (D): Deficiency in glycogen synthase causes GSD 0 (or GSD Ib if the glucose transporter G6PC is also affected). Glycogen synthase is responsible for adding glucose units to the growing glycogen chain (α-1,4 linkages). Deficiency leads to decreased glycogen synthesis, resulting in hypoglycemia and hepatomegaly (due to fat accumulation, not abnormal glycogen). The vignette describes glycogen accumulation, not decreased synthesis, and the specific enzyme deficiency is GBE1.
- (E): Impaired activity of the glucose transporter GLUT2 in hepatocytes is associated with Fanconi-Bickel syndrome. This condition involves impaired glucose and galactose uptake, leading to hepatomegaly (due to glycogen and fat accumulation), renal tubular dysfunction (Fanconi syndrome), and sometimes cataracts. The primary defect is in glucose transport, not glycogen branching, and the glycogen structure is typically normal. This does not match the specific enzyme deficiency (GBE1) or the abnormal glycogen structure (amylopectin-like) found in the patient.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Andersen disease (GSD IV) is caused by GBE1 deficiency, leading to abnormal glycogen accumulation (amylopectin-like) and severe liver cirrhosis. Key features include early onset, progressive liver failure, and characteristic abnormal glycogen structure on biopsy. Differentiate it from other GSDs based on the affected enzyme, glycogen structure, and clinical presentation (e.g., GSD Ia - glucose-6-phosphatase deficiency, normal glycogen; GSD III - debranching enzyme deficiency, limit dextrin accumulation; GSD 0 - glycogen synthase deficiency, decreased glycogen synthesis).

---

<a id='question-084'></a>

### Question 084: Glycogen Storage Disease Type V (McArdle Disease)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of Glycogen Storage Disease Type V (McArdle disease), specifically the deficiency of muscle glycogen phosphorylase (myophosphorylase), its autosomal recessive inheritance, clinical presentation (exercise intolerance, muscle cramps, myoglobinuria), diagnostic biochemical findings (lack of increase in blood lactate during exercise, accumulation of glycogen in muscle biopsy), and the mechanism of action of glucagon and epinephrine on glycogenolysis in different tissues. (考察五型糖原贮积症（麦克阿德尔病），即肌肉糖原磷酸化酶（肌磷酸化酶）缺乏症，其常染色体隐性遗传、临床表现（运动不耐受、肌肉痉挛、肌红蛋白尿）、诊断性生化发现（运动期间血乳酸无升高、肌肉活检中糖原积累）以及胰高血糖素和肾上腺素在不同组织中糖原分解作用的机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department complaining of severe muscle cramps and dark urine following an intense soccer game. He reports a lifelong history of exercise intolerance, experiencing muscle pain and fatigue much earlier than his peers during physical activities. His past medical history is unremarkable, and he denies any significant family history of muscle disorders. Physical examination reveals tender calf muscles. Laboratory tests show elevated serum creatine kinase (CK) levels (15,000 U/L, normal <200 U/L) and myoglobinuria. A muscle biopsy reveals an accumulation of glycogen with normal enzyme histochemical staining for acid maltase (lysosomal alpha-glucosidase) and phosphorylase kinase. During a subsequent exercise tolerance test, his blood lactate levels remain normal and do not increase significantly despite strenuous exercise. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *PYGM* gene.

Which of the following statements best explains the lack of increased blood lactate levels during exercise in this patient?

(A) Glucagon stimulates glycogenolysis in skeletal muscle by activating phosphorylase kinase via a Gs-protein coupled receptor pathway.
(B) Epinephrine stimulates glycogenolysis in skeletal muscle by activating phosphorylase kinase via a Gs-protein coupled receptor pathway.
(C) The deficiency of muscle glycogen phosphorylase prevents the breakdown of glycogen into glucose-1-phosphate, which is subsequently converted to glucose-6-phosphate and then glucose.
(D) The deficiency of muscle glycogen phosphorylase prevents the breakdown of glycogen into glucose-1-phosphate, which is subsequently converted to glucose-6-phosphate, but glucose-6-phosphate cannot be converted to glucose in skeletal muscle due to the lack of glucose-6-phosphatase.
(E) The deficiency of muscle glycogen phosphorylase prevents the breakdown of glycogen into glucose-1-phosphate, which is subsequently converted to glucose-6-phosphate, and the accumulation of glucose-6-phosphate inhibits phosphofructokinase-1, leading to decreased glycolysis and lactate production.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old male with exercise intolerance, muscle cramps, and dark urine (myoglobinuria) after intense exercise. This points towards a muscle disorder exacerbated by activity.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Markedly elevated CK and myoglobinuria indicate muscle damage (rhabdomyolysis).
- **Muscle Biopsy:** Glycogen accumulation with normal acid maltase and phosphorylase kinase staining rules out Pompe disease (GSD II) and glycogen storage disease type IX (phosphorylase kinase deficiency), respectively.
- **Exercise Tolerance Test:** Normal blood lactate levels despite strenuous exercise are a key finding. Normally, during exercise, muscle glycogenolysis and glycolysis increase significantly, leading to a rise in blood lactate. The lack of lactate increase suggests a block in glycogen breakdown or subsequent glycolysis.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in the *PYGM* gene confirms Glycogen Storage Disease Type V (McArdle disease), caused by deficiency of muscle glycogen phosphorylase (myophosphorylase).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the explanation of the lack of increased blood lactate during exercise.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **McArdle Disease (GSD V):** Caused by deficiency of myophosphorylase, the muscle-specific isoform of glycogen phosphorylase. This enzyme catalyzes the rate-limiting step in glycogenolysis in muscle: the cleavage of glucose units from the non-reducing ends of glycogen chains, releasing glucose-1-phosphate.
- **Glycogenolysis in Muscle:** Glycogen (n glucose units) + Pi → Glycogen (n-1 glucose units) + Glucose-1-phosphate.
- **Fate of Glucose-1-Phosphate:** Glucose-1-phosphate is isomerized to Glucose-6-phosphate by phosphoglucomutase.
- **Fate of Glucose-6-Phosphate in Muscle:** In skeletal muscle, Glucose-6-phosphate enters glycolysis to provide ATP for muscle contraction. It cannot be converted to free glucose because muscle lacks the enzyme glucose-6-phosphatase.
- **Mechanism of Lactate Production:** During intense exercise, oxygen supply may become limiting. Under anaerobic conditions, pyruvate (the end product of glycolysis) is converted to lactate by lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) to regenerate NAD+ needed for glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate dehydrogenase in glycolysis, allowing glycolysis to continue and produce ATP.
- **Consequence of Myophosphorylase Deficiency:** The inability to break down glycogen into glucose-1-phosphate prevents the generation of glucose-6-phosphate from glycogen stores during exercise. While muscle can still utilize glucose from the blood, the glycogen stores cannot be mobilized. This leads to early fatigue and cramps. Crucially, the lack of glycogen breakdown means less glucose-6-phosphate is produced via glycogenolysis, leading to reduced flux through glycolysis and consequently, reduced pyruvate and lactate production.
- **Glucagon and Epinephrine:** These hormones stimulate glycogenolysis primarily in the liver (and to some extent, adipose tissue) by activating adenylyl cyclase via Gs-protein coupled receptors, increasing cAMP, activating protein kinase A (PKA), which then phosphorylates and activates phosphorylase kinase, which in turn phosphorylates and activates glycogen phosphorylase. However, skeletal muscle glycogen phosphorylase is primarily regulated by allosteric effectors (AMP, Ca2+) and covalent modification via phosphorylase kinase, but the hormonal regulation pathway (via cAMP/PKA) is less significant or absent in muscle compared to the liver. Even if the hormonal pathway were fully functional in muscle, the deficiency of the enzyme itself (myophosphorylase) would prevent glycogenolysis regardless of hormonal stimulation.
- **Why Lactate Doesn't Increase:** The primary reason lactate doesn't increase is the inability to break down muscle glycogen to provide glucose-6-phosphate for glycolysis. The reduced glycolytic flux leads to decreased pyruvate formation and subsequently decreased lactate production, even though glycolysis from blood glucose can still occur to some extent.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Glucagon primarily acts on the liver, not skeletal muscle, to stimulate glycogenolysis. While it uses a Gs-protein coupled receptor pathway, its effect on muscle glycogenolysis is negligible due to the lack of appropriate receptors or downstream signaling components. Furthermore, even if it did stimulate muscle phosphorylase kinase, the enzyme (myophosphorylase) is deficient. Incorrect.
- (B): Epinephrine does have some effect on muscle glycogenolysis via beta-adrenergic receptors (Gs-protein coupled), but this pathway is less critical than allosteric regulation (AMP, Ca2+) and covalent modification. More importantly, the primary defect is the *absence* of functional myophosphorylase. Even if epinephrine stimulated the regulatory cascade, the enzyme itself is missing or non-functional, so glycogenolysis cannot occur. Incorrect.
- (C): This statement correctly describes the initial step blocked by myophosphorylase deficiency (glycogen breakdown to glucose-1-phosphate). However, it doesn't fully explain why lactate levels don't rise. While glucose-1-phosphate isn't formed, glucose-6-phosphate *can* still be formed from blood glucose uptake and glycolysis. The lack of lactate is due to the overall reduced glycolytic flux originating from glycogen stores, not just the lack of glucose-1-phosphate formation. This option is partially correct but incomplete and doesn't fully capture the reason for low lactate. Incorrect.
- (E): This statement suggests that the accumulation of glucose-6-phosphate inhibits phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK-1). While high levels of G6P can inhibit PFK-1 (via allosteric inhibition by G6P and ATP), this is not the primary reason for low lactate in McArdle disease. The main issue is the *lack* of G6P production from glycogenolysis, leading to reduced overall glycolytic flux, especially during intense exercise when glycogen is the primary fuel source. Incorrect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** McArdle disease (GSD V) is characterized by a deficiency in muscle glycogen phosphorylase, leading to exercise intolerance, cramps, myoglobinuria, and a failure of blood lactate to rise during exercise. Hormonal regulation (glucagon, epinephrine) plays a minimal role in muscle glycogenolysis compared to the liver. The lack of lactate is due to impaired glycogen breakdown, reducing the substrate (G6P) for glycolysis.
- **Differentials:** Other GSDs (e.g., GSD II - Pompe disease, GSD IV - Andersen disease, GSD IX - Cori disease) present differently. Pompe disease involves lysosomal alpha-glucosidase deficiency, leading to glycogen accumulation in lysosomes and muscle weakness. GSD IV involves abnormal glycogen structure (branching enzyme deficiency). GSD IX involves liver phosphorylase deficiency (usually asymptomatic or mild hypoglycemia) or muscle phosphorylase kinase deficiency (similar presentation to GSD V but often milder and responsive to exercise). The key diagnostic feature for GSD V is the failure of lactate to rise post-exercise.

<a id='question-085'></a>

### Question 085: McArdle Disease Symptoms: Exercise Intolerance, Painful Muscle Cramping, Fatigue, 'Second Wind' Phenomenon (After Switching to Free Fatty Acids) - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of McArdle disease (Glycogen Storage Disease Type V), specifically the biochemical basis for its characteristic symptoms during exercise, including the "second wind" phenomenon, related to muscle energy metabolism and the role of myophosphorylase deficiency. (考察麦卡多病（糖原贮积症五型）的生化基础，包括运动期间的特征性症状，如“第二次风”现象，以及肌磷酸化酶缺乏在肌肉能量代谢中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old male presents to the emergency department complaining of severe muscle pain, cramping, and weakness in his legs after a strenuous soccer game. He reports similar episodes occurring after intense physical activity since adolescence. He denies any history of trauma or recent illness. His past medical history is unremarkable, but his family history reveals that his maternal uncle also experienced similar symptoms after exercise. Physical examination shows normal muscle strength at rest, but palpation of the calf muscles reveals tenderness. Laboratory tests show elevated serum creatine kinase (CK) levels (15,000 U/L, normal < 200 U/L), normal serum glucose, lactate, and pyruvate levels immediately after exercise. A muscle biopsy reveals an absence of activity of the muscle phosphorylase enzyme. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *PYGM* gene. The patient is advised to avoid intense exercise and maintain a diet rich in complex carbohydrates. He is also told that if he must exercise, starting slowly and gradually increasing intensity might help him avoid symptoms, and that after an initial period of fatigue, he might experience a "second wind" phenomenon. Which of the following biochemical mechanisms best explains the "second wind" phenomenon observed in this patient?

(A) Increased anaerobic glycolysis leading to rapid ATP production from glucose.
(B) Increased fatty acid oxidation providing ATP via the electron transport chain.
(C) Increased utilization of plasma glucose by the muscle cells.
(D) Increased activity of liver phosphorylase leading to increased blood glucose.
(E) Increased synthesis of glycogen in the muscle cells.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 28-year-old male with exercise-induced muscle pain, cramping, weakness, elevated CK, normal glucose/lactate/pyruvate post-exercise. This points towards a muscle metabolic disorder affecting energy production during exercise.
- **Family History:** Maternal uncle with similar symptoms suggests an inherited disorder, potentially X-linked or autosomal recessive.
- **Muscle Biopsy:** Absence of muscle phosphorylase activity confirms McArdle disease (Glycogen Storage Disease Type V).
- **Genetic Testing:** *PYGM* gene mutation confirms the diagnosis.
- **"Second Wind" Phenomenon:** The question specifically asks for the mechanism behind this phenomenon.
- **Key Clue:** McArdle disease is caused by a deficiency in muscle glycogen phosphorylase (myophosphorylase), which is responsible for breaking down glycogen into glucose-1-phosphate for glycolysis. During initial intense exercise, muscles rely heavily on glycogen stores for ATP. Without functional myophosphorylase, glycogen breakdown is impaired, leading to ATP depletion, lactate accumulation (though lactate is normal *immediately* post-exercise in this vignette, it would typically rise during exercise), muscle cramps, and pain.
- **Second Wind Explanation:** After a period of initial fatigue, the body adapts. Blood flow to the muscles increases, delivering more oxygen and fatty acids. Muscle cells switch from relying primarily on anaerobic glycolysis (which is limited by the lack of glucose-1-phosphate from glycogen) to utilizing fatty acids for ATP production via aerobic respiration (beta-oxidation and oxidative phosphorylation). This shift provides a sustained, albeit slower, source of ATP, allowing the patient to continue exercising, hence the "second wind."

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **McArdle Disease:** Deficiency of muscle glycogen phosphorylase (encoded by *PYGM*). This enzyme catalyzes the rate-limiting step in glycogenolysis in muscle: (Glycogen)n + Pi → G1P + (Glycogen)n-1.
- **Initial Exercise:** Muscle initially relies on stored glycogen for rapid ATP production via glycolysis. In McArdle disease, this pathway is blocked. ATP levels drop quickly, leading to muscle fatigue, cramps, and pain. Anaerobic glycolysis from blood glucose is limited and insufficient to meet the high energy demand.
- **"Second Wind" Mechanism:** As exercise continues, several changes occur:
    1.  **Increased Blood Flow:** Enhanced circulation delivers more oxygen and substrates to the muscle.
    2.  **Increased Fatty Acid Mobilization:** Lipolysis increases, releasing free fatty acids (FFAs) into the bloodstream.
    3.  **Increased FFA Uptake:** Muscle cells increase their uptake of FFAs.
    4.  **Increased FFA Oxidation:** FFAs undergo beta-oxidation in the mitochondria, producing acetyl-CoA.
    5.  **Aerobic Respiration:** Acetyl-CoA enters the Krebs cycle and oxidative phosphorylation, generating large amounts of ATP. This process is slower than glycolysis but provides a more sustained energy supply.
- **Why other options are wrong:**
    -   (A) Anaerobic glycolysis is impaired in McArdle disease due to the lack of glucose-1-phosphate from glycogen breakdown. It cannot provide sufficient ATP.
    -   (C) While plasma glucose is available, muscle cells cannot efficiently utilize it for glycolysis without the initial glucose-1-phosphate provided by glycogenolysis.
    -   (D) Liver phosphorylase is unaffected in McArdle disease. It breaks down liver glycogen to maintain blood glucose levels, which is not the primary mechanism for the second wind in muscle.
    -   (E) Glycogen synthesis (glycogenesis) is the opposite of glycogen breakdown (glycogenolysis) and is not the mechanism for energy production during exercise.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Increased anaerobic glycolysis leading to rapid ATP production from glucose.** Incorrect. Anaerobic glycolysis requires glucose-6-phosphate, which is primarily derived from glycogen breakdown (via glucose-1-phosphate) or uptake of blood glucose. In McArdle disease, glycogenolysis is blocked, severely limiting the substrate for anaerobic glycolysis. While some glucose can be taken up from the blood, it's insufficient for the initial high energy demand, and anaerobic glycolysis alone cannot explain the sustained energy production of the "second wind."
- (B) **Increased fatty acid oxidation providing ATP via the electron transport chain.** Correct. This accurately describes the shift in muscle metabolism during the "second wind" phenomenon in McArdle disease. As glycogen stores cannot be utilized, the muscle adapts by increasing reliance on fatty acid oxidation, a slower but more sustained ATP-generating pathway.
- (C) **Increased utilization of plasma glucose by the muscle cells.** Incorrect. While plasma glucose is available, its utilization via glycolysis is limited in McArdle disease because the initial step requiring glucose-1-phosphate (from glycogen) is blocked. The muscle cannot efficiently break down glycogen to provide the glucose-6-phosphate needed for glycolysis from plasma glucose.
- (D) **Increased activity of liver phosphorylase leading to increased blood glucose.** Incorrect. Liver phosphorylase is not affected in McArdle disease. Its function is to maintain blood glucose levels, not directly provide energy to the affected muscles. While maintaining blood glucose is important, it doesn't explain the energy source for the "second wind" within the muscle itself.
- (E) **Increased synthesis of glycogen in the muscle cells.** Incorrect. Glycogen synthesis (glycogenesis) is the process of storing glucose as glycogen. This occurs when energy levels are high, not during strenuous exercise when energy is being consumed. Furthermore, even if synthesis occurred, it wouldn't provide energy for muscle contraction.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** McArdle disease (muscle glycogen phosphorylase deficiency) impairs glycogenolysis in muscle, leading to exercise intolerance. The "second wind" phenomenon occurs because the muscle shifts its energy metabolism from relying on limited glycogen stores and insufficient glycolysis to utilizing fatty acid oxidation via aerobic respiration.
- **Differentials:** Other glycogen storage diseases (e.g., Cori disease - GSD III, affecting debranching enzyme; Pompe disease - GSD II, affecting lysosomal acid alpha-glucosidase) have different clinical presentations and underlying defects. Mitochondrial myopathies can also cause exercise intolerance but typically involve defects in oxidative phosphorylation, not glycogenolysis. Carnitine deficiency impairs fatty acid transport into mitochondria, also causing exercise intolerance but with a different mechanism.

<a id='question-086'></a>

### Question 086: McArdle Disease Complication
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: McArdle disease (Glycogen Storage Disease Type V) is caused by a deficiency in muscle glycogen phosphorylase (myophosphorylase), leading to impaired glycogenolysis in skeletal muscle. Exercise triggers muscle breakdown (rhabdomyolysis), releasing myoglobin into the circulation, which can cause acute tubular necrosis (ATN) and renal failure. (McArdle 病 (V 型糖原贮积症) 是由于肌肉糖原磷酸化酶 (肌磷酸化酶) 缺乏引起的，导致骨骼肌糖原分解受损。运动会引发肌肉分解 (横纹肌溶解症)，释放肌红蛋白进入循环，可能导致急性肾小管坏死 (ATN) 和肾功能衰竭。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old male presents to the emergency department after experiencing severe muscle cramps and weakness following a strenuous workout. He reports a history of exercise intolerance since childhood, often experiencing muscle pain and fatigue after physical activity. His past medical history is unremarkable, and he denies any significant family history of muscle disorders. On examination, his vital signs are stable, but his urine is described as dark, cola-colored. Laboratory investigations reveal markedly elevated serum creatine kinase (CK) levels (over 10,000 U/L), elevated serum myoglobin, and evidence of acute kidney injury (elevated serum creatinine and BUN). Urinalysis shows positive blood but no red blood cells on microscopy, consistent with myoglobinuria. A muscle biopsy shows an accumulation of glycogen with a lack of phosphorylase activity. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *PYGM* gene encoding myophosphorylase. Which of the following is the most likely mechanism underlying the patient's acute kidney injury?

(A) Deposition of uric acid crystals in the renal tubules due to increased purine catabolism from muscle breakdown.
(B) Direct toxic effect of free hemoglobin released from lysed red blood cells during strenuous exercise.
(C) Precipitation of myoglobin in the renal tubules, leading to obstruction and direct tubular cell toxicity.
(D) Formation of insoluble calcium phosphate complexes in the renal interstitium due to hyperphosphatemia resulting from muscle cell lysis.
(E) Immune complex deposition in the glomeruli triggered by the release of muscle-specific antigens during rhabdomyolysis.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 28-year-old male with exercise intolerance since childhood, muscle cramps/weakness after strenuous activity. This points towards a metabolic myopathy.
- **Symptoms:** Severe muscle cramps, weakness after exercise. Classic symptoms of McArdle disease.
- **Urine Appearance:** Dark, cola-colored urine. Highly suggestive of myoglobinuria, which occurs when muscle breakdown (rhabdomyolysis) releases myoglobin into the bloodstream, which is then filtered by the kidneys.
- **Lab Findings:**
    - Markedly elevated CK (>10,000 U/L): Indicates significant muscle damage/necrosis (rhabdomyolysis).
    - Elevated serum myoglobin: Confirms release of myoglobin from damaged muscle.
    - Acute kidney injury (elevated creatinine/BUN): Indicates renal dysfunction.
    - Urinalysis: Positive blood, no RBCs. This is characteristic of myoglobinuria; myoglobin reacts positively with the dipstick test for heme (like hemoglobin), but microscopic examination shows no red blood cells.
- **Muscle Biopsy:** Glycogen accumulation, lack of phosphorylase activity. Diagnostic for McArdle disease (Glycogen Storage Disease Type V).
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous *PYGM* mutation. Confirms the diagnosis of McArdle disease, caused by deficiency of muscle glycogen phosphorylase (myophosphorylase).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism of acute kidney injury (AKI). The combination of rhabdomyolysis (high CK, myoglobinuria) and AKI strongly suggests myoglobin-induced acute tubular necrosis (ATN).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **McArdle Disease:** Deficiency of myophosphorylase (encoded by *PYGM*). Myophosphorylase is the muscle-specific isoenzyme of glycogen phosphorylase. Its function is to break down glycogen into glucose-1-phosphate in muscle cells, providing glucose for energy during exercise.
- **Pathophysiology:** Without functional myophosphorylase, muscle cells cannot efficiently utilize stored glycogen for energy during exercise. This leads to ATP depletion, muscle cramps, pain, weakness, and fatigue.
- **Rhabdomyolysis:** Severe exercise in McArdle patients can cause significant muscle fiber damage and necrosis (rhabdomyolysis).
- **Myoglobin Release:** Damaged muscle cells release large amounts of myoglobin into the bloodstream.
- **Myoglobinuria:** Myoglobin is filtered by the glomeruli. In large quantities, it overwhelms the reabsorptive capacity of the proximal renal tubules.
- **Acute Tubular Necrosis (ATN):** Myoglobin is nephrotoxic. Several mechanisms contribute to ATN:
    - **Direct Toxicity:** Myoglobin can directly damage proximal tubular epithelial cells.
    - **Tubular Obstruction:** Myoglobin can precipitate within the tubules, particularly in acidic urine, causing physical obstruction.
    - **Oxygen Radical Formation:** Myoglobin heme can catalyze the formation of reactive oxygen species (ROS), leading to oxidative stress and cellular damage.
    - **Vasoconstriction:** Myoglobin can cause renal vasoconstriction, reducing blood flow to the tubules.
- **Acute Kidney Injury (AKI):** The combination of tubular obstruction and direct toxicity leads to ATN, resulting in decreased glomerular filtration rate (GFR) and AKI, characterized by elevated serum creatinine and BUN.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Uric acid crystals:** While severe muscle breakdown can lead to increased purine turnover and potentially hyperuricemia, the primary cause of AKI in this scenario is myoglobinuria, not uric acid nephropathy (gout). Uric acid nephropathy typically occurs in chronic hyperuricemia or tumor lysis syndrome, not acutely from exercise-induced rhabdomyolysis. The vignette specifically points to myoglobinuria (cola-colored urine, positive blood dipstick without RBCs).
- (B): **Free hemoglobin:** Hemoglobin is released from *red blood cells* (hemolysis), not muscle cells (rhabdomyolysis). While hemolysis can also cause AKI via similar mechanisms (hemoglobinuria), the clinical picture (muscle cramps after exercise, elevated CK, myoglobinuria) clearly indicates rhabdomyolysis as the source of the pigment in the urine.
- (D): **Calcium phosphate complexes:** Muscle cell lysis releases phosphate, potentially leading to hyperphosphatemia. However, the primary mechanism of AKI in myoglobinuria is direct tubular toxicity and obstruction by myoglobin itself, not precipitation of calcium phosphate complexes in the interstitium. Hyperphosphatemia is a consequence of rhabdomyolysis but not the direct cause of the ATN in this context.
- (E): **Immune complex deposition:** Immune complex deposition typically causes glomerulonephritis, affecting the glomeruli, not primarily the tubules as seen in ATN. While inflammation can occur, the main pathology in myoglobin-induced AKI is direct tubular damage and obstruction. There is no indication of an immune-mediated process in the vignette.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
McArdle disease (muscle glycogen phosphorylase deficiency) leads to exercise-induced rhabdomyolysis, releasing myoglobin into the circulation. High levels of filtered myoglobin can cause acute tubular necrosis (ATN) and acute kidney injury (AKI) due to direct tubular toxicity and precipitation within the tubules. Key differentials for AKI include prerenal azotemia, postrenal obstruction, and intrinsic renal disease (like ATN from other causes, glomerulonephritis, interstitial nephritis). In the context of rhabdomyolysis, myoglobinuria is the hallmark cause of ATN.

<a id='question-087'></a>

### Question 087: McArdle Disease - Forearm Exercise Test Interpretation
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the biochemical basis of McArdle disease (Glycogen Storage Disease Type V, myophosphorylase deficiency) and its characteristic findings in a non-ischemic forearm exercise test, specifically the absence of lactate rise and the presence of an exaggerated ammonia rise. (理解迈克阿德尔病（糖原贮积症 V 型，肌磷酸化酶缺乏症）的生化基础及其在非缺血性前臂运动试验中的特征性发现，特别是乳酸水平不升高和氨水平异常升高。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old male presents to the clinic complaining of exercise intolerance, muscle cramps, and myoglobinuria after strenuous physical activity. He reports that these symptoms began in his late teens and have progressively worsened. His past medical history is unremarkable, and he denies any significant family history of similar symptoms. Physical examination reveals normal muscle strength and bulk at rest. A non-ischemic forearm exercise test is performed. The patient exercises his forearm by repeatedly clenching and opening his fist. Blood samples are collected before exercise, during exercise, and after exercise. The results show a normal increase in blood ammonia levels during exercise, which is significantly higher than expected for the level of exertion. However, there is no increase in blood lactate levels during or after exercise compared to baseline levels. Muscle biopsy reveals a deficiency in myophosphorylase activity. Which of the following biochemical abnormalities best explains the exaggerated rise in ammonia during forearm exercise in this patient?

(A) Impaired urea cycle function due to accumulation of alanine.
(B) Increased utilization of branched-chain amino acids (BCAAs) for energy production in the absence of glycogenolysis.
(C) Increased breakdown of purines due to ATP depletion.
(D) Decreased activity of lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) due to muscle damage.
(E) Increased glycolysis through the pentose phosphate pathway leading to increased NADPH production and ammonia detoxification.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 28-year-old male with exercise intolerance, cramps, myoglobinuria since late teens. This points towards a metabolic myopathy.
- **Forearm Exercise Test:** Normal increase in ammonia, but *no* increase in lactate. This is the key finding. Lactate is produced from pyruvate during anaerobic glycolysis, which is the primary energy source during intense exercise when oxygen supply is limited. The absence of lactate rise indicates a block in glycolysis *before* pyruvate formation, specifically glycogenolysis or the initial steps of glycolysis.
- **Muscle Biopsy:** Deficiency in myophosphorylase activity confirms the diagnosis of McArdle disease (Glycogen Storage Disease Type V). Myophosphorylase catalyzes the rate-limiting step of glycogenolysis in muscle (glycogen -> glucose-1-phosphate -> glucose-6-phosphate).
- **Question:** Why is ammonia elevated during exercise in this patient? Ammonia is a byproduct of amino acid catabolism.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **McArdle Disease:** Deficiency of muscle glycogen phosphorylase (myophosphorylase). This enzyme breaks down glycogen into glucose-1-phosphate, which is then converted to glucose-6-phosphate, entering glycolysis.
- **Exercise in McArdle:** Without functional myophosphorylase, muscle cannot efficiently break down glycogen for energy during exercise. Glycogen accumulates in muscle.
- **Energy Source Shift:** Since glycogenolysis is blocked, the muscle must rely on alternative fuel sources, primarily fatty acids (via beta-oxidation) and amino acids.
- **Amino Acid Catabolism:** Increased reliance on amino acids for energy leads to increased protein breakdown and catabolism. The catabolism of branched-chain amino acids (BCAAs - leucine, isoleucine, valine) is particularly important for energy production in muscle.
- **Ammonia Production:** The breakdown of amino acids, especially BCAAs, releases ammonia (NH3). The liver normally detoxifies ammonia into urea via the urea cycle. However, during intense exercise, the rate of ammonia production from muscle can exceed the liver's capacity to detoxify it, leading to a rise in blood ammonia.
- **Exaggerated Ammonia Rise:** In McArdle disease, the reliance on amino acids for energy is significantly increased due to the inability to use glycogen. This leads to a disproportionately high rate of amino acid catabolism and ammonia production during exercise, resulting in an exaggerated rise in blood ammonia levels, even though lactate (from glycolysis) does not rise.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired urea cycle function due to accumulation of alanine. Alanine is a gluconeogenic amino acid, and its accumulation is not the primary issue in McArdle disease. The primary problem is increased BCAA catabolism, not alanine accumulation or urea cycle impairment. While high ammonia can stress the urea cycle, the *cause* of the high ammonia is the increased amino acid breakdown, not a primary urea cycle defect.
- (B): Increased utilization of branched-chain amino acids (BCAAs) for energy production in the absence of glycogenolysis. This is the correct explanation. The lack of glycogen breakdown forces the muscle to use alternative fuels, including BCAAs. BCAA catabolism releases ammonia. Increased BCAA utilization -> Increased ammonia production -> Exaggerated ammonia rise.
- (C): Increased breakdown of purines due to ATP depletion. While ATP is depleted in McArdle disease during exercise, the primary source of ammonia during exercise is amino acid catabolism, not purine breakdown. Purine breakdown contributes to ammonia, but it's not the main driver of the *exaggerated* rise seen in this context.
- (D): Decreased activity of lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) due to muscle damage. LDH converts pyruvate to lactate. Decreased LDH activity would *impair* lactate production, which is consistent with the lack of lactate rise. However, it does not explain the *exaggerated* ammonia rise. Muscle damage (indicated by myoglobinuria) can release LDH into the blood, but the *lack* of lactate rise is due to the block in glycogenolysis/glycolysis, not decreased LDH activity itself.
- (E): Increased glycolysis through the pentose phosphate pathway leading to increased NADPH production and ammonia detoxification. The pentose phosphate pathway (PPP) is an alternative glucose metabolism pathway. In McArdle disease, glycolysis is impaired due to the block in glycogenolysis, so flux through the PPP would likely be *decreased*, not increased. Furthermore, the PPP does not directly lead to ammonia detoxification; ammonia detoxification primarily occurs in the liver via the urea cycle.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
McArdle disease (myophosphorylase deficiency) blocks glycogenolysis in muscle. During exercise, this leads to a lack of lactate production (no glycogen -> no glycolysis -> no pyruvate -> no lactate) and forces the muscle to rely on alternative fuels like fatty acids and amino acids. Increased amino acid catabolism, particularly BCAAs, results in an exaggerated rise in blood ammonia during the non-ischemic forearm exercise test. Differential diagnoses for exercise intolerance include other glycogen storage diseases (e.g., Pompe disease - acid maltase deficiency, Cori disease - debranching enzyme deficiency, McArdle disease), mitochondrial myopathies, and lipid storage diseases. The specific pattern of no lactate rise with exaggerated ammonia rise is characteristic of McArdle disease.

<a id='question-088'></a>

### Question 088: Glycogen Storage Disease Type VI (Hers Disease)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of Glycogen Storage Disease Type VI (Hers disease), specifically the deficiency of liver glycogen phosphorylase, its clinical presentation (mild hepatomegaly, ketotic hypoglycemia), biochemical findings (normal muscle glycogen phosphorylase activity, normal blood glucose after exercise), and the underlying molecular defect.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 5-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with lethargy, vomiting, and pallor after an overnight fast. His mother reports similar episodes occurring several times a year, often triggered by skipping meals or prolonged periods without eating. Physical examination reveals mild hepatomegaly but no splenomegaly or muscle weakness. Vital signs show a heart rate of 110 bpm, blood pressure of 95/60 mmHg, and respiratory rate of 24/min. Laboratory investigations reveal hypoglycemia (blood glucose 45 mg/dL), elevated ketones in urine, and normal serum lactate. Liver function tests are normal. A muscle biopsy is performed, and glycogen phosphorylase activity is measured in both liver and muscle tissue. The assay shows significantly reduced glycogen phosphorylase activity in the liver homogenate (10% of normal) but normal activity in the muscle homogenate (95% of normal). Glycogen content in the liver biopsy is increased, appearing as large, pale cytoplasmic vacuoles on histopathology. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *PYGL* gene. The boy is subsequently challenged with a 12-hour fast. His blood glucose level remains stable at 70 mg/dL after 12 hours, and glycogen phosphorylase activity in muscle tissue remains normal after the fast. Which of the following statements best explains the normal blood glucose level and normal muscle glycogen phosphorylase activity after a 12-hour fast in this patient?

(A) The liver's reduced glycogen phosphorylase activity is compensated for by increased gluconeogenesis, and muscle glycogen phosphorylase is unaffected because it is encoded by a different gene (*PYGM*).
(B) The liver's reduced glycogen phosphorylase activity is compensated for by increased uptake of glucose from the portal circulation, and muscle glycogen phosphorylase is unaffected because it is regulated by an allosteric inhibitor specific to the liver isoform.
(C) The liver's reduced glycogen phosphorylase activity is compensated for by increased lipolysis and fatty acid oxidation, and muscle glycogen phosphorylase is unaffected because it is encoded by a different gene (*PYGM*) and is not subject to the same regulatory mechanisms as the liver isoform.
(D) The liver's reduced glycogen phosphorylase activity is compensated for by increased fructose-1,6-bisphosphatase activity, and muscle glycogen phosphorylase is unaffected because it is encoded by a different gene (*PYGL*) and is not subject to the same regulatory mechanisms as the liver isoform.
(E) The liver's reduced glycogen phosphorylase activity is compensated for by increased glycogen synthase activity, and muscle glycogen phosphorylase is unaffected because it is encoded by a different gene (*PYGM*) and is not subject to the same regulatory mechanisms as the liver isoform.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 5-year-old boy with recurrent episodes of lethargy, vomiting, pallor after fasting, triggered by skipping meals. This suggests fasting hypoglycemia.
*   **Physical Exam:** Mild hepatomegaly, no splenomegaly or muscle weakness. Hepatomegaly points towards liver involvement; lack of muscle weakness argues against generalized glycogen storage diseases affecting muscle significantly.
*   **Lab Findings:** Hypoglycemia (45 mg/dL), elevated ketones (ketotic hypoglycemia), normal lactate. This pattern is characteristic of impaired glucose production (glycogenolysis or gluconeogenesis) during fasting, leading to ketone body formation as an alternative fuel source. Normal lactate rules out mitochondrial disorders or defects in gluconeogenesis involving pyruvate dehydrogenase or lactate dehydrogenase.
*   **Enzyme Assay:** Significantly reduced liver glycogen phosphorylase activity (10% of normal), normal muscle glycogen phosphorylase activity (95% of normal). This is the key diagnostic finding, indicating a tissue-specific defect in liver glycogen phosphorylase.
*   **Histopathology:** Increased liver glycogen content with large, pale cytoplasmic vacuoles. Consistent with impaired glycogen breakdown.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in the *PYGL* gene. This gene encodes the liver isoform of glycogen phosphorylase.
*   **Fasting Challenge:** Stable blood glucose (70 mg/dL) after 12 hours, normal muscle glycogen phosphorylase activity. This demonstrates that muscle glycogenolysis is functioning normally, maintaining blood glucose, while the liver defect persists.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the explanation of normal blood glucose and normal muscle glycogen phosphorylase activity after fasting.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Glycogen Storage Disease Type VI (Hers Disease):** Caused by a deficiency of liver glycogen phosphorylase (EC 2.4.1.1). This enzyme catalyzes the rate-limiting step in glycogenolysis, breaking down glycogen into glucose-1-phosphate.
*   **Molecular Basis:** The liver isoform (Glycogen Phosphorylase b) is encoded by the *PYGL* gene. The muscle isoform (Glycogen Phosphorylase b) is encoded by the *PYGM* gene. These are distinct genes located on different chromosomes (PYGL on 6q12, PYGM on 14q22.3).
*   **Tissue Specificity:** The defect in Hers disease is specific to the liver isoform due to mutations in the *PYGL* gene. The muscle isoform, encoded by *PYGM*, is unaffected.
*   **Clinical Manifestations:** Patients typically present with mild hepatomegaly and fasting hypoglycemia (often ketotic). Muscle weakness is usually absent because muscle glycogenolysis is normal.
*   **Biochemical Findings:** Low liver glycogen phosphorylase activity, normal muscle glycogen phosphorylase activity.
*   **Fasting Response:** During fasting, the liver normally breaks down glycogen to maintain blood glucose. In Hers disease, this process is impaired in the liver. However, muscle glycogenolysis remains intact. Muscle glycogen breakdown provides glucose-6-phosphate, which can enter glycolysis and contribute to ATP production within the muscle cell. Importantly, muscle glycogenolysis does *not* directly release free glucose into the bloodstream for systemic use (muscle lacks glucose-6-phosphatase). However, the question stem states blood glucose remains stable after 12 hours. This stability is maintained primarily by gluconeogenesis in the liver and kidneys, which is not directly affected by the glycogen phosphorylase defect. The liver can still produce glucose via gluconeogenesis from precursors like lactate, alanine, and glycerol. Furthermore, the body can utilize fatty acids and ketone bodies as alternative fuels, reducing the reliance on hepatic glucose output.
*   **Why Muscle Activity is Normal:** Muscle glycogen phosphorylase is encoded by the *PYGM* gene, which is unaffected in Hers disease. It functions normally to provide glucose-6-phosphate for muscle energy needs during fasting and exercise.
*   **Why Blood Glucose is Stable (after 12 hours):** Although liver glycogenolysis is impaired, gluconeogenesis remains functional. The body adapts to utilize alternative fuels (fatty acids, ketones). The 12-hour fast duration might not be long enough to completely deplete gluconeogenic substrates or overwhelm the compensatory mechanisms. The stability is primarily due to intact gluconeogenesis and alternative fuel utilization, not direct glucose release from muscle glycogen.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. While the liver's reduced activity is compensated for (primarily by gluconeogenesis and alternative fuels), stating that muscle glycogen phosphorylase is unaffected *because* it is encoded by a different gene (*PYGM*) is true, but the compensation mechanism mentioned (increased gluconeogenesis) is incomplete and doesn't fully explain the stable blood glucose in the context of impaired liver glycogenolysis. The primary compensation is gluconeogenesis and fat/ketone utilization, not just gluconeogenesis alone. Also, the statement implies gluconeogenesis *directly* compensates for the *lack* of glycogenolysis, which is partially true but less precise than stating gluconeogenesis maintains glucose production independently.
- (B): Incorrect. Increased uptake of glucose from the portal circulation is not the primary compensatory mechanism during fasting. During fasting, portal glucose uptake decreases. Also, while the liver isoform has specific regulation, stating it's due to an allosteric inhibitor specific to the liver isoform is incorrect; the regulation involves phosphorylation/dephosphorylation and allosteric effectors (like AMP, glucose-6-phosphate, caffeine) that affect both isoforms, although their sensitivity might differ.
- (D): Incorrect. Increased fructose-1,6-bisphosphatase activity is part of gluconeogenesis, which *does* compensate. However, stating that muscle glycogen phosphorylase is unaffected because it is encoded by a different gene (*PYGL*) is factually wrong; it's encoded by *PYGM*. Also, the statement implies that increased fructose-1,6-bisphosphatase *directly* compensates for the lack of glycogenolysis, which is an oversimplification. Gluconeogenesis as a whole compensates.
- (E): Incorrect. Increased glycogen synthase activity would lead to *increased* glycogen storage, not compensation for impaired glycogen breakdown. Glycogen synthase is involved in glycogen synthesis, not breakdown. Furthermore, stating that muscle glycogen phosphorylase is unaffected because it is encoded by a different gene (*PYGM*) is true, but the compensation mechanism mentioned (increased glycogen synthase) is completely incorrect for explaining stable blood glucose during fasting in a glycogenolysis defect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Glycogen Storage Disease Type VI (Hers disease) is characterized by a deficiency of the *liver* isoform of glycogen phosphorylase (*PYGL* gene), leading to fasting hypoglycemia and mild hepatomegaly, but *normal* muscle function due to an intact muscle isoform (*PYGM* gene). Compensation for impaired liver glycogenolysis during fasting relies primarily on intact gluconeogenesis and utilization of alternative fuels (fatty acids, ketones). Key differentials include other GSDs (like Type I, III, V affecting the liver) and other causes of fasting hypoglycemia (e.g., insulinoma, fatty acid oxidation defects). Remember the tissue specificity and the distinct genes (*PYGL* vs. *PYGM*) for the liver and muscle isoforms.

<a id='question-089'></a>

### Question 089: Glycogen Storage Disease Type VII (Tarui Disease)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of Glycogen Storage Disease Type VII (Tarui disease), caused by a deficiency in muscle phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK-M), its clinical presentation mimicking McArdle disease (GSD V) but with additional features like hemolytic anemia, and the underlying biochemical mechanism. (考察糖原贮积症VII型（Tarui病），由肌肉磷酸果糖激酶-1 (PFK-M) 缺乏引起，其临床表现类似于McArdle病（GSD V），但具有溶血性贫血等额外特征，以及潜在的生化机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old male presents to the emergency department after experiencing severe muscle cramps, myoglobinuria (dark urine), and fatigue following intense exercise. He reports a lifelong history of exercise intolerance, with symptoms typically starting within minutes of strenuous activity. His past medical history is unremarkable, and he denies any family history of similar symptoms or muscle disorders. Physical examination reveals normal muscle strength at rest but tenderness upon palpation of the calf muscles. Laboratory investigations show elevated serum creatine kinase (CK) levels (15,000 U/L, normal <200 U/L) and myoglobinuria. A muscle biopsy is performed. Histochemical analysis of the muscle biopsy shows normal glycogen stores, but enzyme activity assays reveal a significant deficiency (approximately 10% of normal) in phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK-1) activity specifically in muscle tissue. Red blood cell (RBC) enzyme assays show normal levels of pyruvate kinase and glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD). However, osmotic fragility testing of his RBCs is markedly increased. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *PFKM* gene, which encodes the muscle-specific isoform of PFK-1.

Which of the following biochemical mechanisms BEST explains the patient's hemolytic anemia in addition to his exercise-induced muscle symptoms?

(A) Deficiency of pyruvate kinase in erythrocytes leads to decreased ATP production, causing RBC membrane instability and hemolysis during exercise-induced acidosis.
(B) Deficiency of glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) in erythrocytes leads to decreased NADPH production, impairing the pentose phosphate pathway and increasing oxidative stress, resulting in hemolysis.
(C) Deficiency of PFK-M in erythrocytes leads to decreased ATP production via glycolysis, causing RBC membrane instability and hemolysis, particularly exacerbated by exercise-induced acidosis and increased ATP demand.
(D) Deficiency of adenylate kinase in erythrocytes leads to decreased AMP recycling and ATP levels, causing RBC membrane instability and hemolysis.
(E) Deficiency of hexokinase in erythrocytes leads to decreased glucose phosphorylation and ATP production, causing RBC membrane instability and hemolysis.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 28-year-old male with exercise intolerance, muscle cramps, myoglobinuria, fatigue after exercise. This points towards a disorder affecting muscle energy metabolism during exertion, similar to McArdle disease (GSD V).
- **Lab Findings:** Markedly elevated CK and myoglobinuria confirm rhabdomyolysis.
- **Muscle Biopsy:** Normal glycogen stores rule out GSDs involving glycogen accumulation (like GSD V, Pompe, Cori, Hers). Deficiency of muscle PFK-1 activity confirms Glycogen Storage Disease Type VII (Tarui disease).
- **RBC Findings:** Normal pyruvate kinase and G6PD rule out deficiencies in these enzymes as the cause of hemolysis. Increased osmotic fragility suggests RBC membrane instability.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in *PFKM* gene confirms the diagnosis of GSD VII, affecting the muscle isoform (PFK-M).
- **Key Deduction:** The question asks for the mechanism of *hemolytic anemia* in this patient with confirmed GSD VII (muscle PFK-M deficiency). This requires understanding that GSD VII affects *both* muscle and red blood cells because PFK-1 is crucial for glycolysis in both tissues.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Glycogen Storage Disease Type VII (Tarui Disease):** Caused by deficiency of the *PFKM* gene, encoding the muscle isoform of phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK-M). PFK-1 catalyzes the phosphorylation of fructose-6-phosphate to fructose-1,6-bisphosphate, a key regulatory and committed step in glycolysis. This reaction requires ATP and Mg2+.
- **Muscle Manifestations:** In muscle, PFK-M deficiency impairs glycolysis, limiting ATP production during anaerobic exercise. This leads to energy depletion, muscle cramps, pain, fatigue, rhabdomyolysis (elevated CK, myoglobinuria), and exercise intolerance, mimicking McArdle disease (GSD V, myophosphorylase deficiency).
- **RBC Manifestations:** PFK-1 is also essential for glycolysis in erythrocytes, which rely solely on glycolysis for ATP production. Deficiency of PFK-1 in RBCs (due to the systemic nature of the *PFKM* mutation, although expression levels differ) leads to decreased ATP synthesis. ATP is crucial for maintaining RBC shape and membrane integrity via the Na+/K+-ATPase pump. Low ATP levels impair this pump, leading to ion imbalances (Na+ influx, K+ efflux), cell swelling, and increased osmotic fragility. During exercise, acidosis develops, further impairing glycolysis and ATP production in already compromised RBCs, exacerbating hemolysis.
- **Mechanism of Hemolysis:** The decreased ATP production due to PFK-M deficiency in erythrocytes directly compromises the ATP-dependent ion pumps (Na+/K+-ATPase, Ca2+-ATPase) responsible for maintaining osmotic balance and membrane integrity. This leads to membrane instability and premature destruction of RBCs (hemolysis), especially under conditions of increased ATP demand or acidosis (like during exercise).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Pyruvate kinase deficiency causes hemolytic anemia due to decreased ATP production in RBCs, but this patient has normal pyruvate kinase levels. This is a common cause of hereditary non-spherocytic hemolytic anemia, but it's ruled out by the lab data.
- (B): G6PD deficiency causes hemolytic anemia due to increased oxidative stress, leading to Heinz body formation and RBC damage. This patient has normal G6PD levels. This is the most common enzyme deficiency causing hemolytic anemia, but it's ruled out by the lab data.
- (D): Adenylate kinase deficiency is rare and primarily affects muscle, causing exercise intolerance and cramps, but it is not typically associated with hemolytic anemia. Adenylate kinase is involved in nucleotide salvage (interconverting AMP, ADP, ATP), but its deficiency doesn't directly cause the profound ATP depletion seen in PFK-M deficiency leading to RBC membrane instability.
- (E): Hexokinase deficiency is also rare and primarily affects RBCs, causing hemolytic anemia due to decreased glucose phosphorylation and ATP production. While it causes hemolysis due to ATP deficiency, the patient's specific diagnosis is GSD VII (PFK-M deficiency), and the question asks for the mechanism related to *this* specific condition. PFK-M deficiency is the established cause of both muscle and RBC symptoms in this patient.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Glycogen Storage Disease Type VII (Tarui disease) is caused by muscle PFK-1 deficiency (*PFKM* gene mutation). It presents with exercise intolerance and muscle symptoms similar to McArdle disease (GSD V) but is distinguished by hemolytic anemia due to impaired glycolysis and ATP production in erythrocytes. Remember that PFK-1 is crucial for glycolysis in both muscle and RBCs. Key differentials for exercise-induced muscle symptoms include GSD V (McArdle), GSD VII (Tarui), phosphofructokinase deficiency (liver isoform), and myoadenylate deaminase deficiency. Key differentials for hemolytic anemia include G6PD deficiency, pyruvate kinase deficiency, hereditary spherocytosis, and sickle cell anemia. GSD VII uniquely combines muscle and RBC manifestations due to the systemic effect of the PFK-M deficiency on glycolysis.

### Question 090: Glycogen Storage Disease Type 0 (GSD0)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Glycogen synthase deficiency (GSD0) impairs glycogen synthesis, leading to fasting hypoglycemia due to inability to store glucose as glycogen and postprandial hyperglycemia due to impaired glucose uptake and storage after meals. (糖原合成酶缺乏症 (GSD0) 损害糖原合成，导致禁食性低血糖，因为无法将葡萄糖储存为糖原，以及餐后高血糖，因为餐后葡萄糖摄取和储存受损。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with recurrent episodes of lethargy, sweating, and confusion, particularly after fasting overnight or during periods of increased physical activity. His mother reports that he often becomes irritable and shaky in the morning before breakfast. She also notes that after large carbohydrate-rich meals, he sometimes experiences transient symptoms like nausea and abdominal discomfort. His past medical history is unremarkable, and there is no known family history of metabolic disorders. Physical examination reveals a slightly thin child with normal vital signs (BP 100/60 mmHg, HR 80 bpm, RR 18/min, Temp 37°C). Laboratory investigations during a hypoglycemic episode show a blood glucose level of 45 mg/dL (normal fasting range 70-100 mg/dL), elevated serum lactate (3.5 mmol/L, normal < 2.0 mmol/L), and normal liver function tests. A subsequent oral glucose tolerance test (OGTT) reveals a rapid rise in blood glucose to 180 mg/dL at 30 minutes post-glucose load, followed by a slower decline compared to age-matched controls, with a peak glucose level of 220 mg/dL at 60 minutes, and a return to near-normal levels by 120 minutes. A liver biopsy shows normal glycogen content but reveals markedly reduced glycogen synthase activity in the liver tissue homogenate. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *GSS* gene encoding liver glycogen synthase.

Which of the following biochemical mechanisms best explains the patient's postprandial hyperglycemia following a glucose load?

(A) Impaired gluconeogenesis in the liver due to decreased availability of pyruvate resulting from lactate accumulation.
(B) Reduced hepatic uptake of glucose from the portal circulation due to decreased activity of glucokinase.
(C) Decreased activity of glycogen synthase, preventing the efficient conversion of glucose-6-phosphate to glycogen, leading to increased intracellular glucose concentration and subsequent glucose release into the bloodstream.
(D) Increased activity of hepatic phosphorylase, leading to excessive glycogenolysis and glucose release into the bloodstream.
(E) Impaired insulin secretion from pancreatic beta cells due to chronic hypoglycemia, leading to reduced glucose uptake by peripheral tissues.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 6-year-old boy with recurrent lethargy, sweating, confusion after fasting (hypoglycemia) and irritability/nausea after large meals (hyperglycemia). This pattern suggests a defect in glucose storage or utilization.
- **Lab Findings:** Fasting hypoglycemia (45 mg/dL) confirms impaired glucose release from stores. Elevated lactate (3.5 mmol/L) suggests increased anaerobic glycolysis, possibly due to impaired glucose utilization or shunting of glucose-6-phosphate. Normal liver function tests rule out significant liver damage.
- **OGTT Results:** Rapid initial rise (180 mg/dL at 30 min) followed by a slower decline and a peak of 220 mg/dL at 60 min, returning to near-normal by 120 min. This pattern is characteristic of GSD0: glucose enters the liver but cannot be efficiently stored as glycogen, leading to hyperglycemia initially, followed by a slower return to baseline as glucose is slowly metabolized or released.
- **Liver Biopsy:** Normal glycogen content (contradicting GSDs involving glycogen breakdown or branching) but reduced glycogen synthase activity confirms the diagnosis of GSD0.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous *GSS* mutation confirms the molecular basis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism of *postprandial hyperglycemia*.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **GSD0 Pathophysiology:** Glycogen synthase is the key enzyme responsible for polymerizing UDP-glucose into glycogen chains. Deficiency leads to impaired glycogen synthesis.
- **Fasting Hypoglycemia:** The liver cannot store excess glucose as glycogen after a meal. During fasting, the liver cannot break down stored glycogen to release glucose into the blood, leading to hypoglycemia.
- **Postprandial Hyperglycemia:** After a glucose load, glucose enters the liver via GLUT2 transporters and is phosphorylated by glucokinase to glucose-6-phosphate (G6P). In a normal liver, G6P is primarily converted to UDP-glucose by phosphoglucomutase and then polymerized into glycogen by glycogen synthase. In GSD0, glycogen synthase activity is deficient. This leads to an accumulation of G6P and its precursor, glucose, within the hepatocytes. The high intracellular glucose concentration drives glucose efflux from the liver via GLUT2 back into the bloodstream, causing postprandial hyperglycemia. The impaired glycogen synthesis also means less glucose is sequestered into glycogen, further contributing to hyperglycemia. The elevated lactate may result from increased glycolysis of the accumulated G6P that cannot enter the glycogen synthesis pathway.
- **Molecular Basis:** The *GSS* gene encodes the catalytic subunit of liver glycogen synthase. Mutations typically lead to reduced enzyme activity or stability.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired gluconeogenesis is not the primary mechanism. Gluconeogenesis is the synthesis of glucose from non-carbohydrate precursors (lactate, alanine, glycerol). While lactate is elevated, this is likely a consequence of impaired glucose utilization/storage, not the cause of hyperglycemia. Gluconeogenesis is typically *increased* during fasting in GSD0 to compensate for the lack of glycogenolysis, but it doesn't explain the *postprandial* hyperglycemia.
- (B): Reduced hepatic uptake of glucose is incorrect. Glucokinase activity is generally normal in GSD0. The problem is not uptake but storage. Glucose uptake via GLUT2 is driven by the concentration gradient, and the high blood glucose after a meal ensures uptake occurs.
- (D): Increased activity of hepatic phosphorylase is incorrect. Phosphorylase is the enzyme responsible for glycogenolysis (glycogen breakdown). Its activity is *decreased* or functionally irrelevant because there is little or no glycogen to break down in GSD0. Furthermore, increased phosphorylase activity would lead to hypoglycemia, not hyperglycemia.
- (E): Impaired insulin secretion is incorrect. While chronic hypoglycemia can sometimes affect insulin secretion, the primary defect in GSD0 is hepatic. The hyperglycemia is due to the liver's inability to store glucose, not a lack of insulin response. Insulin is still secreted in response to the high glucose levels, but the liver cannot effectively respond by storing glycogen.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Glycogen Storage Disease Type 0 (GSD0) is caused by a deficiency in glycogen synthase, leading to impaired glycogen synthesis. This results in fasting hypoglycemia (due to inability to store glucose) and postprandial hyperglycemia (due to inability to store ingested glucose, leading to its efflux from the liver). Key differential diagnoses include other GSDs (e.g., GSD I, III, IV, VI) which present with different patterns of hypoglycemia and/or hepatomegaly, and defects in glucose transport (e.g., GLUT2 deficiency). The OGTT pattern in GSD0 is distinct: initial hyperglycemia followed by a slower decline.
