<a id='question-100'></a>

### Question 100: Glycogen Storage Disease Type III (Cori / Forbes Disease)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Defect in debranching enzyme (amylo-1,6-glucosidase) leads to accumulation of abnormal glycogen (limit dextrin) with short outer branches, causing hepatomegaly, hypoglycemia, hyperlipidemia, and muscle weakness, but typically *without* significant cardiomegaly or severe hypotonia seen in Type II. (淀粉样糖原病 III 型 (Cori/Forbes 病) 是由于脱支酶 (α-1,6-葡萄糖苷酶) 缺陷导致，引起异常糖原 (限支淀粉) 积累，表现为肝脏肿大、低血糖、高脂血症和肌肉无力，但通常*不*伴有显著的心脏肥大或严重的肌张力低下，如 II 型。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the pediatrician with recurrent episodes of hypoglycemia, particularly during overnight fasting. His mother reports that he tires easily during play and has experienced several episodes of vomiting and abdominal pain. Physical examination reveals hepatomegaly (liver palpable 5 cm below the right costal margin) and mild hypotonia. His growth has been slightly below average for his age. Laboratory investigations show fasting hypoglycemia (glucose 45 mg/dL), elevated serum triglycerides (450 mg/dL), elevated liver enzymes (AST 180 U/L, ALT 150 U/L), and normal creatine kinase (CK). A liver biopsy reveals diffuse glycogen accumulation within hepatocytes, visualized by PAS staining. Electron microscopy shows abnormal glycogen rosettes with short, sparse outer branches. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *AGL* gene.

Which of the following biochemical abnormalities is most likely responsible for the patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Deficiency of glycogen phosphorylase, leading to impaired glycogenolysis and accumulation of normal glycogen structure.
(B) Deficiency of the debranching enzyme (amylo-1,6-glucosidase), resulting in the accumulation of limit dextrin, which is glycogen with short outer branches.
(C) Deficiency of glucose-6-phosphatase, causing impaired gluconeogenesis and glycogenolysis, leading to accumulation of normal glycogen structure and severe fasting hypoglycemia.
(D) Deficiency of phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK-1), resulting in impaired glycolysis and accumulation of fructose-1,6-bisphosphate, causing hemolytic anemia and muscle pain.
(E) Deficiency of pyruvate dehydrogenase complex, leading to impaired pyruvate metabolism and accumulation of pyruvate and lactate, causing lactic acidosis and neurological dysfunction.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Age & Symptoms:** 3-year-old boy with recurrent fasting hypoglycemia, easy fatigability, vomiting, abdominal pain. These point towards a metabolic disorder affecting glucose homeostasis, likely involving glycogen metabolism or gluconeogenesis.
- **Physical Exam:** Hepatomegaly (liver enlarged) and mild hypotonia. Hepatomegaly suggests glycogen or lipid accumulation in the liver. Hypotonia can be seen in some metabolic disorders.
- **Lab Findings:** Fasting hypoglycemia (glucose 45 mg/dL), elevated triglycerides (450 mg/dL), elevated liver enzymes (AST/ALT). Hypoglycemia confirms impaired glucose production/release. Hypertriglyceridemia is common in GSDs due to altered lipid metabolism. Elevated liver enzymes indicate liver damage/stress. Normal CK rules out significant muscle breakdown (less likely Type II GSD).
- **Liver Biopsy:** Diffuse glycogen accumulation (PAS positive), abnormal glycogen rosettes with short outer branches (electron microscopy). This is the key finding pointing to abnormal glycogen structure.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in the *AGL* gene. The *AGL* gene encodes the debranching enzyme (amylo-1,6-glucosidase).
- **Deduction:** The combination of fasting hypoglycemia, hepatomegaly, abnormal glycogen structure (short outer branches), and a mutation in the *AGL* gene strongly indicates Glycogen Storage Disease Type III (Cori/Forbes disease), caused by debranching enzyme deficiency. The abnormal glycogen structure, known as limit dextrin, is the direct result of this enzyme deficiency.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Glycogen Metabolism:** Glycogen is synthesized by glycogen synthase, adding glucose units via α-1,4 linkages to form linear chains. Branching enzyme (amylo-(1,4→1,6)-transglycosylase) creates branches via α-1,6 linkages every 8-12 glucose units.
- **Glycogenolysis:** Glycogen phosphorylase cleaves glucose units from the non-reducing ends via α-1,4 linkages, releasing glucose-1-phosphate. This continues until it reaches a branch point (4 glucose units away from the α-1,6 linkage).
- **Debranching Enzyme:** The debranching enzyme has two activities:
    1.  **Transferase activity:** Transfers a block of 3 glucose residues from the branch to the non-reducing end of another chain, leaving a single glucose residue attached via an α-1,6 linkage.
    2.  **α-1,6-glucosidase activity:** Hydrolyzes the remaining single glucose residue attached by the α-1,6 linkage, releasing free glucose.
- **GSD Type III:** Deficiency of the debranching enzyme (amylo-1,6-glucosidase) prevents the complete breakdown of glycogen at branch points. Glycogen phosphorylase can still act on the outer chains, but the resulting limit dextrin (glycogen with short outer branches, 4 glucose units long) accumulates in the liver and muscle. This abnormal glycogen is poorly soluble and less effective at maintaining blood glucose levels during fasting. The *AGL* gene encodes the debranching enzyme.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** Accumulation of limit dextrin leads to hepatomegaly, fasting hypoglycemia, hyperlipidemia (due to impaired glucose metabolism and increased fatty acid synthesis), and muscle weakness (though typically less severe than Type II). Cardiomegaly is usually absent. The clinical picture matches the vignette.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Deficiency of glycogen phosphorylase causes GSD Type VI (Hers disease). This leads to impaired glycogenolysis, accumulation of *normal* glycogen structure, hepatomegaly, mild hypoglycemia, and hyperlipidemia. The key difference is the *normal* glycogen structure, unlike the limit dextrin seen in the vignette.
- (C): Deficiency of glucose-6-phosphatase causes GSD Type Ia (von Gierke disease). This enzyme is crucial for the final step of both glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis (converting glucose-6-phosphate to free glucose). Deficiency leads to severe fasting hypoglycemia, hepatomegaly (due to glycogen and fat accumulation), lactic acidosis, hyperuricemia, and hyperlipidemia. Glycogen structure is normal. The vignette describes abnormal glycogen structure and lacks lactic acidosis/hyperuricemia.
- (D): Deficiency of phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK-1) causes a glycolytic defect, primarily affecting red blood cells (hemolytic anemia) and muscle (exercise intolerance, myalgia). It does not directly cause glycogen storage disease or abnormal glycogen accumulation in the liver. Fructose-1,6-bisphosphate accumulates.
- (E): Deficiency of the pyruvate dehydrogenase complex causes impaired conversion of pyruvate to acetyl-CoA, linking glycolysis to the TCA cycle. This leads to lactic acidosis (pyruvate is converted to lactate), neurological dysfunction (brain relies heavily on glucose/acetyl-CoA), and accumulation of pyruvate. It does not cause glycogen storage disease or abnormal glycogen accumulation.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Glycogen Storage Disease Type III (Cori/Forbes disease) is caused by a deficiency in the debranching enzyme (amylo-1,6-glucosidase), leading to the accumulation of limit dextrin (glycogen with short outer branches). Key features include hepatomegaly, fasting hypoglycemia, hyperlipidemia, and muscle weakness, but typically *without* significant cardiomegaly or severe hypotonia. Differentiate from GSD Type II (Pompe disease, lysosomal α-glucosidase deficiency, causing cardiomegaly and hypotonia), GSD Type Ia (von Gierke disease, glucose-6-phosphatase deficiency, causing severe hypoglycemia and lactic acidosis), and GSD Type VI (Hers disease, glycogen phosphorylase deficiency, causing normal glycogen structure).
